It describes such individuals as trading Allah’s revelations for a small worldly gain. The consequence of this act is described in powerful imagery: they are said to "consume nothing but fire" in their bellies. On the Day of Resurrection, Allah will neither speak to them nor purify them, signifying total rejection and severe punishment. This verse underscores the gravity of tampering with divine truth and the serious consequences that await those who do so for material benefit.

Verse 175

أُولَـٰئِكَ الَّذِينَ اشْتَرَوُا الضَّلَالَةَ بِالْهُدَىٰ وَالْعَذَابَ بِالْمَغْفِرَةِ ۚ فَمَا أَصْبَرَهُمْ عَلَى النَّارِ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Continuing the theme of the previous verse, this one highlights the terrible choice made by those who conceal or distort Allah’s revelations. Their action is described as a deliberate transaction in which they exchange Allah’s clear guidance for misguidance and error. Furthermore, they give up the opportunity for Allah’s forgiveness in return for a path that leads to suffering and torment. The verse concludes with a striking exclamation about their audacity—choosing a course that leads directly to the Fire (Hell). This underscores the immense loss they bring upon themselves by rejecting divine guidance.

Verse 176

ذَٰلِكَ بِأَنَّ اللَّهَ نَزَّلَ الْكِتَابَ بِالْحَقِّ ۗ وَإِنَّ الَّذِينَ اخْتَلَفُوا فِي الْكِتَابِ لَفِي شِقَاقٍ بَعِيدٍ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse explains the reason for the severe outcome described in the previous verses. It states that the punishment awaits those who reject guidance and distort truth because Allah sent down His Book with truth and without doubt. However, instead of accepting this truth, some people actively look for ways to dispute or create disagreements within it. This behavior leads them into a state of deep division. The verse highlights that rejecting or disputing the clear truth of Allah's revealed word leads to being fundamentally misguided and distant from the intended path.

Verse 177

لَّيْسَ الْبِرَّ أَن تُوَلُّوا وُجُوهَكُمْ قِبَلَ الْمَشْرِقِ وَالْمَغْرِبِ وَلَـٰكِنَّ الْبِرَّ مَنْ آمَنَ بِاللَّهِ وَالْيَوْمِ الْآخِرِ وَالْمَلَائِكَةِ وَالْكِتَابِ وَالنَّبِيِّينَ وَآتَى الْمَالَ عَلَىٰ حُبِّهِ ذَوِي الْقُرْبَىٰ وَالْيَتَامَىٰ وَالْمَسَاكِينَ وَابْنَ السَّبِيلِ وَالسَّائِلِينَ وَفِي الرِّقَابِ وَأَقَامَ الصَّلَاةَ وَآتَى الزَّكَاةَ وَالْمُوفُونَ بِعَهْدِهِمْ إِذَا عَاهَدُوا ۖ وَالصَّابِرِينَ فِي الْبَأْسَاءِ وَالضَّرَّاءِ وَحِينَ الْبَأْسِ ۗ أُولَـٰئِكَ الَّذِينَ صَدَقُوا ۖ وَأُولَـٰئِكَ هُمُ الْمُتَّقُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This concluding verse provides a comprehensive definition of true righteousness. It states clearly that righteousness is not merely about physical acts like facing a certain direction during prayer. Instead, it is grounded in core beliefs: faith in Allah, the Last Day (day of judgment), the angels, all the divine Books, and all of Allah's Messengers. Following these beliefs, true righteousness manifests in significant actions: spending one's wealth (even though it is cherished) to help relatives, orphans, the poor, stranded travelers, those who ask for help, and for freeing captives. It also includes consistently performing prayer and giving *zakat* (a form of mandatory charity), fulfilling promises and contracts, and demonstrating firmness and patience during difficult times, suffering, and fear. The verse concludes by identifying those who embody these beliefs and actions as the "people of truth" and those who are truly conscious of Allah and fear displeasing Him. This verse summarizes the essence of faith and good conduct as a unified path to righteousness, combining inner conviction with outward action for the benefit of oneself and society.

Verse 178

يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا كُتِبَ عَلَيْكُمُ الْقِصَاصُ فِي الْقَتْلَى ۖ الْحُرُّ بِالْحُرِّ وَالْعَبْدُ بِالْعَبْدِ وَالْأُنثَىٰ بِالْأُنثَىٰ ۚ فَمَنْ عُفِيَ لَهُ مِنْ أَخِيهِ شَيْءٌ فَاتِّبَاعٌ بِالْمَعْرُوفِ وَأَدَاءٌ إِلَيْهِ بِإِحْسَانٍ ۗ ذَٰلِكَ تَخْفِيفٌ مِّن رَّبِّكُمْ وَرَحْمَةٌ ۗ فَمَنِ اعْتَدَىٰ بَعْدَ ذَٰلِكَ فَلَهُ عَذَابٌ أَلِيمٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse instructs believers on the principle of qisās—just retribution—in cases of unlawful killing. It emphasizes proportional justice, originally expressed as a free person for a free person, a slave for a slave, and a woman for a woman, reflecting the legal norms of that time. Yet, Allah’s mercy offers a compassionate alternative: if the victim’s family chooses to forgive and accept diyah (financial compensation), this path is both legitimate and honorable. In such cases, the family should seek compensation with fairness, and the offender must pay it promptly and respectfully. This option serves to prevent further bloodshed and encourage reconciliation. The verse warns that any violation of this agreement—through injustice or renewed aggression—will bring severe consequences. Ultimately, this law reflects a balance between justice and mercy, central to building a morally upright and orderly society.

Verse 179

وَلَكُمْ فِي الْقِصَاصِ حَيَاةٌ يَا أُولِي الْأَلْبَابِ لَعَلَّكُمْ تَتَّقُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Following the previous verse which outlines the law of just retribution (qisas), this verse explains the profound wisdom behind it. It teaches that this law, seemingly about taking a life, actually serves to preserve life for the community as a whole. It achieves this by acting as a strong deterrent against murder; when someone knows the penalty for taking a life is the loss of their own, they are more likely to refrain. Furthermore, by allowing the victim's family justice or compensation, it prevents cycles of endless revenge and vendettas that could lead to many more deaths, as historically happened in some societies. The verse calls upon people of understanding to reflect on this wisdom. Ultimately, the aim is to encourage self-restraint and God-consciousness (taqwa), which acts as an inner safeguard against committing such crimes in the first place and against seeking excessive revenge. This verse highlights how divine law benefits humanity by promoting both justice and the sanctity of life, reinforcing the connection between social order and individual accountability to Allah.

Verse 180

كُتِبَ عَلَيْكُمْ إِذَا حَضَرَ أَحَدَكُمُ الْمَوْتُ إِن تَرَكَ خَيْرًا الْوَصِيَّةُ لِلْوَالِدَيْنِ وَالْأَقْرَبِينَ بِالْمَعْرُوفِ ۖ حَقًّا عَلَى الْمُتَّقِينَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Continuing the theme of social responsibilities, this verse provides guidance regarding personal wills and inheritance. It states that when a Muslim is nearing death and has property, it is a duty to make a will or bequest (wasiyyah). This will should be in favor of parents and close relatives, to be managed according to fair and reasonable customs. At the time this verse was revealed, specific shares of inheritance had not yet been mandated. Later verses in the Qur'an established fixed shares for parents and other close relatives, making them legal heirs who receive inheritance directly by law. Therefore, this instruction came to apply primarily to relatives who are *not* direct legal heirs under Islamic inheritance law. Making a will for such relatives is seen as a way to strengthen family ties and ensure that those who might otherwise receive nothing are also provided for, reflecting compassion and fairness. This obligation is described as a duty for those who are God-conscious (Allah-fearing), linking proper handling of wealth and family responsibilities to one's relationship with Allah.

Verse 181

فَمَن بَدَّلَهُ بَعْدَ مَا سَمِعَهُ فَإِنَّمَا إِثْمُهُ عَلَى الَّذِينَ يُبَدِّلُونَهُ ۚ إِنَّ اللَّهَ سَمِيعٌ عَلِيمٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse addresses the integrity of the will mentioned in the previous verse. It warns that if a person who has knowledge of a will changes its contents after the death of the person who made it, the sin and responsibility for that alteration fall entirely upon the one who makes the change. The deceased person, having fulfilled their duty by making the will, bears no blame for subsequent unauthorized modifications. The verse reinforces this by stating that Allah hears everything and knows everything. This means Allah is aware of the original intentions of the testator, the actions of those who tamper with the will, and all hidden matters. Muslims believe that Allah's attributes, like hearing and knowing all things, are affirmed as they appear in the Qur'an, without likening Allah to His creation or explaining how they occur. Rather, they reflect His majesty and uniqueness in a way beyond human comprehension. This serves as a powerful reminder of accountability to Allah, who is the ultimate witness and judge of people's actions, especially concerning fulfilling trusts and upholding justice regarding the deceased's property.

Verse 182

فَمَنْ خَافَ مِن مُّوصٍ جَنَفًا أَوْ إِثْمًا فَأَصْلَحَ بَيْنَهُمْ فَلَا إِثْمَ عَلَيْهِ ۚ إِنَّ اللَّهَ غَفُورٌ رَّحِيمٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: While the previous verse firmly prohibits altering a will, this verse offers important clarification regarding situations where fairness may be at stake. If the executor sincerely believes that the testator made an error or acted unjustly—perhaps by favoring certain heirs over others—and takes steps to mediate and guide the outcome toward a more equitable and peaceful resolution, they are not considered sinful. When such action is motivated by the desire to uphold justice and prevent conflict among family members, it is not only permitted but viewed positively. This reflects Islam’s broader moral framework: preserving family harmony, addressing potential wrongs, and fostering compassion. The verse concludes by affirming that Allah is Most Forgiving, Most Merciful, highlighting that striving for justice and reconciliation, even in delicate matters like inheritance, aligns with divine mercy and guidance. As with the rulings on retribution and bequests, this guidance ultimately points back to the core values of God-consciousness and ethical responsibility.

Verse 183

يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا كُتِبَ عَلَيْكُمُ الصِّيَامُ كَمَا كُتِبَ عَلَى الَّذِينَ مِن قَبْلِكُمْ لَعَلَّكُمْ تَتَّقُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse transitions from social and legal matters to a significant religious practice: fasting. It addresses believers directly, highlighting their identity in faith as the basis for accepting this command. It states that fasting has been made obligatory for them, just as it was for communities before them. This indicates that fasting is a universal form of worship with historical roots in monotheistic traditions. The primary purpose given for fasting is to achieve self-restraint or God-consciousness (taqwa). Fasting involves abstaining from food, drink, and other permissible actions during specific hours, which helps individuals gain control over their desires and habits. This discipline strengthens one's will power and increases awareness of Allah, making a person more mindful of His commands and prohibitions in all aspects of life. This connection to taqwa links the practice of fasting back to the overall theme of righteousness and awareness of Allah's presence that runs through this section of the Qur'an.

Verse 184

أَيَّامًا مَّعْدُودَاتٍ ۚ فَمَن كَانَ مِنكُم مَّرِيضًا أَوْ عَلَىٰ سَفَرٍ فَعِدَّةٌ مِّنْ أَيَّامٍ أُخَرَ ۚ وَعَلَى الَّذِينَ يُطِيقُونَهُ فِدْيَةٌ طَعَامُ مِسْكِينٍ ۖ فَمَن تَطَوَّعَ خَيْرًا فَهُوَ خَيْرٌ لَّهُ ۚ وَأَن تَصُومُوا خَيْرٌ لَّكُمْ ۖ إِن كُنتُمْ تَعْلَمُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Building on the previous verse, this explains some initial details about the obligation of fasting. It clarifies that fasting is required for a specific, limited number of days, not continuously. It then provides exemptions: if a person is ill or on a journey, they are excused from fasting during that time but are required to make up the missed days later when they are well or have returned home. This shows Allah's concern for easing hardship. For those for whom fasting is excessively difficult even when not ill or traveling (such as the elderly or those with chronic illnesses), an alternative is provided: compensating by feeding one needy person for each day of fasting missed. The verse also encourages voluntary good deeds beyond the minimum requirement, stating that feeding more than the minimum is better. Finally, it emphasizes the greater benefit of fasting itself, encouraging believers to understand that fasting is ultimately good for them, hinting at its spiritual, disciplinary, and possibly physical advantages. These details illustrate the flexibility and compassion within the divine command, ensuring that the core purpose of attaining God-consciousness remains accessible.

Verse 185

شَهْرُ رَمَضَانَ الَّذِي أُنزِلَ فِيهِ الْقُرْآنُ هُدًى لِّلنَّاسِ وَبَيِّنَاتٍ مِّنَ الْهُدَىٰ وَالْفُرْقَانِ ۚ فَمَن شَهِدَ مِنكُمُ الشَّهْرَ فَلْيَصُمْهُ ۖ وَمَن كَانَ مَرِيضًا أَوْ عَلَىٰ سَفَرٍ فَعِدَّةٌ مِّنْ أَيَّامٍ أُخَرَ ۗ يُرِيدُ اللَّهُ بِكُمُ الْيُسْرَ وَلَا يُرِيدُ بِكُمُ الْعُسْرَ وَلِتُكْمِلُوا الْعِدَّةَ وَلِتُكَبِّرُوا اللَّهَ عَلَىٰ مَا هَدَاكُمْ وَلَعَلَّكُمْ تَشْكُرُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse specifies the month during which the obligatory fasting, mentioned in the previous verses, is to be observed: Ramadan. It highlights the significance of Ramadan as the month in which the Qur'an was first revealed. The Qur'an is described as a guidance for all humanity, containing clear proofs of that guidance and the criterion for distinguishing between right and wrong. Because of this profound connection to the divine revelation, Muslims are commanded to fast the entire month if they are present (not ill or traveling). The exemption for the ill and the traveler, requiring them to make up the days later, is reiterated. The verse then beautifully states a core principle of Islamic law: "Allah intends ease for you; He does not want to put you to difficulties." This confirms that religious obligations are meant to be manageable, not excessively burdensome. The purposes of completing the fast are then given: to fulfill the prescribed number of days, to glorify Allah for having guided believers through the Qur'an and Islam, and to express gratitude for this guidance and all of Allah's favors. This verse underscores the spiritual depth of fasting, linking it to the blessing of the Qur'an and the cultivation of gratitude and awareness of Allah.

Verse 186

وَإِذَا سَأَلَكَ عِبَادِي عَنِّي فَإِنِّي قَرِيبٌ ۖ أُجِيبُ دَعْوَةَ الدَّاعِ إِذَا دَعَانِ ۖ فَلْيَسْتَجِيبُوا لِي وَلْيُؤْمِنُوا بِي لَعَلَّهُمْ يَرْشُدُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Placed within the verses detailing the rules of fasting, this verse offers immense comfort and reassurance. When Prophet Muhammad's followers ask about Allah, Allah Himself provides the answer directly: "I am indeed close (to them)." This emphasizes Allah's immediate accessibility to His servants, without any intermediaries. The verse adds that Allah listens to and answers the prayer (du'a) of everyone who calls upon Him. This signifies Allah's constant attention and responsiveness to the sincere supplications of His servants, especially relevant during times of worship like fasting. In return, Allah calls upon His servants to respond to Him and believe in Him. Responding to Allah means obeying His commands and prohibitions, including those related to fasting and prayer. Believing in Him is the foundation of faith. The verse explains that by responding to Allah and believing in Him, people will be guided to the right way. This intimate dialogue highlights the personal connection available to every believer and encourages them to turn to Allah in prayer, confident in His closeness and readiness to respond, which is particularly empowering during the discipline of fasting.

Verse 187

أُحِلَّ لَكُمْ لَيْلَةَ الصِّيَامِ الرَّفَثُ إِلَىٰ نِسَائِكُمْ ۚ هُنَّ لِبَاسٌ لَّكُمْ وَأَنتُمْ لِبَاسٌ لَّهُنَّ ۗ عَلِمَ اللَّهُ أَنَّكُمْ كُنتُمْ تَخْتَانُونَ أَنفُسَكُمْ فَتَابَ عَلَيْكُمْ وَعَفَا عَنكُمْ ۖ فَالْآنَ بَاشِرُوهُنَّ وَابْتَغُوا مَا كَتَبَ اللَّهُ لَكُمْ ۚ وَكُلُوا وَاشْرَبُوا حَتَّىٰ يَتَبَيَّنَ لَكُمُ الْخَيْطُ الْأَبْيَضُ مِنَ الْخَيْطِ الْأَسْوَدِ مِنَ الْفَجْرِ ۖ ثُمَّ أَتِمُّوا الصِّيَامَ إِلَى اللَّيْلِ ۚ وَلَا تُبَاشِرُوهُنَّ وَأَنتُمْ عَاكِفُونَ فِي الْمَسَاجِدِ ۗ تِلْكَ حُدُودُ اللَّهِ فَلَا تَقْرَبُوهَا ۗ كَذَٰلِكَ يُبَيِّنُ اللَّهُ آيَاتِهِ لِلنَّاسِ لَعَلَّهُمْ يَتَّقُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse provides further practical details about the rules of fasting in Ramadan, specifically concerning the nights. It clarifies that during the nights of the fasting month (between sunset and dawn), it is permissible for married individuals to have sexual intimacy with their spouses. It uses a beautiful metaphor to describe the relationship between spouses: "They are your garments, and you are their garments." Just as a garment provides covering, protection, and comfort, spouses provide the same for each other. The verse acknowledges that before this rule was clarified, some Muslims may have had the misconception that during the fasting period, all sexual intercourse should be avoided—even at night, when eating and drinking are allowed. This verse removes that misconception. It is now explicitly permitted to be intimate and to "seek what Allah has ordained," which includes seeking offspring through this relationship. The verse also defines the time for eating and drinking during Ramadan: it is permitted until the true dawn becomes clearly distinguishable from the darkness of night. The fast then begins at dawn and lasts until nightfall (sunset). A specific prohibition is mentioned for those observing i'tikaf (spiritual retreat, usually in a mosque): sexual intimacy with spouses is forbidden during this period of intense devotion. The verse concludes by emphasizing that these are limits set by Allah, and believers should not even approach these limits but rather maintain a safe distance from anything that might lead to transgression. This serves as a reminder to respect divine boundaries and, like the instruction to fast itself, is given so that people may increase their self-restraint and God-consciousness (taqwa).

Verse 188

وَلَا تَأْكُلُوا أَمْوَالَكُم بَيْنَكُم بِالْبَاطِلِ وَتُدْلُوا بِهَا إِلَى الْحُكَّامِ لِتَأْكُلُوا فَرِيقًا مِّنْ أَمْوَالِ النَّاسِ بِالْإِثْمِ وَأَنتُمْ تَعْلَمُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse follows the discussion on fasting and maintaining a close relationship with Allah by addressing matters of social conduct and financial honesty. It prohibits Muslims from unjustly consuming each other's wealth or property. The verse specifically warns against using wealth to bribe judges or using deceptive tactics in legal proceedings to unjustly take what rightfully belongs to others, even if it seems like a small amount. It emphasizes doing this "wrongfully and knowingly," highlighting the deliberate nature of the injustice. This instruction connects directly to the themes of setting boundaries and being mindful of Allah, which were mentioned in the preceding verses about fasting. It shows that righteousness and fearing Allah (*taqwa*) involve not only performing religious rituals but also upholding justice and honesty in dealings with others, particularly regarding property and legal rights. It underscores that accountability is not just to human judges, but ultimately to Allah, who knows everything. As the Prophet Muhammad taught, while a judge might rule based on outward evidence, if someone takes something that is not rightfully theirs, they are accepting a severe consequence from Allah. This illustrates that ethical behavior and social justice are integral parts of a Muslim's faith and relationship with Allah.

Verse 189

يَسْأَلُونَكَ عَنِ الْأَهِلَّةِ ۖ قُلْ هِيَ مَوَاقِيتُ لِلنَّاسِ وَالْحَجِّ ۗ وَلَيْسَ الْبِرُّ بِأَن تَأْتُوا الْبُيُوتَ مِن ظُهُورِهَا وَلَـٰكِنَّ الْبِرَّ مَنِ اتَّقَىٰ ۗ وَأْتُوا الْبُيُوتَ مِنْ أَبْوَابِهَا ۚ وَاتَّقُوا اللَّهَ لَعَلَّكُمْ تُفْلِحُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse addresses questions about the new moons and their purpose. It explains that the phases of the moon serve as markers for time, helping people determine fixed periods for their activities and for religious duties like the Pilgrimage (Hajj). Following this, the verse dismisses a pre-Islamic custom where people would enter their houses from the rear on certain occasions, particularly after pilgrimage, believing it was a sign of righteousness. The verse clarifies that true righteousness is not about such outward forms or superstitions, but about having consciousness and fear of Allah. It instructs people to enter houses normally, through their proper doors, and reiterates that fearing Allah leads to prosperity. This connects the practical function of the moon phases for Hajj timing with the principle that true piety is inward, centered on awareness of Allah, rather than outward, empty gestures.

Verse 190

وَقَاتِلُوا فِي سَبِيلِ اللَّهِ الَّذِينَ يُقَاتِلُونَكُمْ وَلَا تَعْتَدُوا ۚ إِنَّ اللَّهَ لَا يُحِبُّ الْمُعْتَدِينَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Following the discussion of timekeeping and piety, this verse introduces the topic of fighting. It gives permission to fight in the cause of Allah against those who initiate aggression by fighting you. However, it immediately sets a crucial limit, stating that one must not transgress or exceed proper bounds in fighting, because Allah does not love those who do so. This might have been among the first verses revealed concerning fighting in Islam, establishing the principle of fighting in self-defense against those who initiate hostilities, while emphasizing ethical restrictions.

Verse 191

وَاقْتُلُوهُمْ حَيْثُ ثَقِفْتُمُوهُمْ وَأَخْرِجُوهُم مِّنْ حَيْثُ أَخْرَجُوكُمْ ۚ وَالْفِتْنَةُ أَشَدُّ مِنَ الْقَتْلِ ۚ وَلَا تُقَاتِلُوهُمْ عِندَ الْمَسْجِدِ الْحَرَامِ حَتَّىٰ يُقَاتِلُوكُمْ فِيهِ ۖ فَإِن قَاتَلُوكُمْ فَاقْتُلُوهُمْ ۗ كَذَٰلِكَ جَزَاءُ الْكَافِرِينَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Continuing the rules of engagement introduced in the previous verse, this verse permits fighting and driving out those who had persecuted Muslims and driven them from their homes. It highlights that "tumult and oppression" (or suppression of faith) are considered worse than killing, justifying the defensive action against those who had committed such acts. This perspective reflects the gravity of religious persecution: forcing someone to abandon their faith (oppression) is a severe violation of a fundamental human right and is considered a more serious offense than murder. However, a specific restriction is mentioned: fighting is not allowed near the Sacred Mosque in Makkah unless the enemy initiates combat there. If attacked at the Sacred Mosque, defensive fighting is permitted.

Verse 192

فَإِنِ انتَهَوْا فَإِنَّ اللَّهَ غَفُورٌ رَّحِيمٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse follows directly from the previous one and offers a condition for the cessation of hostilities. It states that if the enemies cease their aggression and oppression, Allah is greatly forgiving and merciful.

Verse 193

وَقَاتِلُوهُمْ حَتَّىٰ لَا تَكُونَ فِتْنَةٌ وَيَكُونَ الدِّينُ لِلَّهِ ۖ فَإِنِ انتَهَوْا فَلَا عُدْوَانَ إِلَّا عَلَى الظَّالِمِينَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Building on the preceding verses, this verse outlines the deeper objective of fighting in Islam: to bring an end to any system of coercion or domination that prevents people from obeying and worshiping Allah freely and exclusively. The term fitnah here refers to a state in which human beings impose their authority over others in place of God's sovereignty. The verse instructs that fighting must continue until such oppressive control is dismantled and the religion—meaning obedience and submission—is sincerely and solely for Allah. However, if the aggressors desist from their hostility and no longer seek to enforce man-made authority over God's law, then fighting must also cease. Hostility is to be directed only against those who persist in wrongdoing and oppression.

Verse 194

الشَّهْرُ الْحَرَامُ بِالشَّهْرِ الْحَرَامِ وَالْحُرُمَاتُ قِصَاصٌ ۚ فَمَنِ اعْتَدَىٰ عَلَيْكُمْ فَاعْتَدُوا عَلَيْهِ بِمِثْلِ مَا اعْتَدَىٰ عَلَيْكُمْ ۚ وَاتَّقُوا اللَّهَ وَاعْلَمُوا أَنَّ اللَّهَ مَعَ الْمُتَّقِينَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse addresses fighting during the sacred months. It establishes a principle of reciprocity, particularly regarding violations of sanctities such as the sacred months. If an enemy transgresses by fighting during a prohibited time or in a sacred area, the Muslim community is permitted to respond in kind. However, this permission is balanced with a strong command to fear Allah and to remember that Allah is with those who exercise self-restraint—emphasizing that retaliation must be measured and kept within limits.

Verse 195

وَأَنفِقُوا فِي سَبِيلِ اللَّهِ وَلَا تُلْقُوا بِأَيْدِيكُمْ إِلَى التَّهْلُكَةِ ۛ وَأَحْسِنُوا ۛ إِنَّ اللَّهَ يُحِبُّ الْمُحْسِنِينَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Following the verses on fighting, this verse urges believers to spend their wealth generously in the cause of Allah. "Spending in the way of Allah" refers to making financial sacrifices to help establish His religion. Neglecting this duty out of attachment to personal interests can lead to ruin in both this world and the Hereafter. Such neglect invites both divine punishment and worldly consequences, including weakness in the face of opposition. The verse warns that miserliness not only harms individuals but also weakens the community’s ability to uphold the faith. It concludes by calling believers to strive for excellence through ihsan—doing good with sincerity and devotion, always mindful that Allah sees all actions.

Verse 196

وَأَتِمُّوا الْحَجَّ وَالْعُمْرَةَ لِلَّهِ ۚ فَإِنْ أُحْصِرْتُمْ فَمَا اسْتَيْسَرَ مِنَ الْهَدْيِ ۖ وَلَا تَحْلِقُوا رُءُوسَكُمْ حَتَّىٰ يَبْلُغَ الْهَدْيُ مَحِلَّهُ ۚ فَمَن كَانَ مِنكُم مَّرِيضًا أَوْ بِهِ أَذًى مِّن رَّأْسِهِ فَفِدْيَةٌ مِّن صِيَامٍ أَوْ صَدَقَةٍ أَوْ نُسُكٍ ۚ فَإِذَا أَمِنتُمْ فَمَن تَمَتَّعَ بِالْعُمْرَةِ إِلَى الْحَجِّ فَمَا اسْتَيْسَرَ مِنَ الْهَدْيِ ۚ فَمَن لَّمْ يَجِدْ فَصِيَامُ ثَلَاثَةِ أَيَّامٍ فِي الْحَجِّ وَسَبْعَةٍ إِذَا رَجَعْتُمْ ۗ تِلْكَ عَشَرَةٌ كَامِلَةٌ ۗ ذَٰلِكَ لِمَن لَّمْ يَكُنْ أَهْلُهُ حَاضِرِي الْمَسْجِدِ الْحَرَامِ ۚ وَاتَّقُوا اللَّهَ وَاعْلَمُوا أَنَّ اللَّهَ شَدِيدُ الْعِقَابِ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Transitioning from earlier themes of fighting and charitable spending, this verse introduces detailed rules concerning the pilgrimage to Mecca—both the major pilgrimage (Hajj) and the lesser pilgrimage (‘Umrah). It instructs believers to complete these sacred journeys once they have been undertaken, and to do so sincerely for the sake of God. If a pilgrim is prevented from completing the rites—due to illness, obstruction, or other valid reasons—a sacrificial animal should be offered instead. In such cases, the pilgrim is allowed to exit the state of consecration (known as ihram, a special spiritual state with physical and behavioral restrictions) without completing all the rites. However, pilgrims should not shave their heads, a symbolic act of completion, until the sacrifice reaches its destination. An exception is made for those suffering from illness or a condition affecting the head, who may shave during ihram, but they must offer compensation—either by fasting, feeding the poor, or making a sacrifice. For those performing the combined pilgrimage (Tamattu', where both ‘Umrah and Hajj are performed in one trip during the Hajj season), a sacrifice is also required. However, if someone cannot afford this, they must fast ten days—three during the pilgrimage and seven after returning home. This rule specifically applies to those who do not live near the Sacred Mosque in Mecca. The verse ends with a reminder to fear God and remain mindful of His strict accountability, underlining the importance of properly fulfilling these religious obligations.

Verse 197

الْحَجُّ أَشْهُرٌ مَّعْلُومَاتٌ ۚ فَمَن فَرَضَ فِيهِنَّ الْحَجَّ فَلَا رَفَثَ وَلَا فُسُوقَ وَلَا جِدَالَ فِي الْحَجِّ ۗ وَمَا تَفْعَلُوا مِنْ خَيْرٍ يَعْلَمْهُ اللَّهُ ۗ وَتَزَوَّدُوا فَإِنَّ خَيْرَ الزَّادِ التَّقْوَىٰ ۚ وَاتَّقُونِ يَا أُولِي الْأَلْبَابِ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Continuing the discussion on Hajj from the previous verse, this verse states that the pilgrimage takes place during well-known, designated months. During this sacred time, specific behavioral guidelines apply. Pilgrims are instructed to avoid lewdness—including sexual activity, suggestive behavior, and even flirtatious speech—sinful conduct, and disputes. Even lawful intimacy between spouses must be avoided during the pilgrimage, reflecting the seriousness and spiritual focus of the journey. While some of these behaviors may be acceptable outside of Hajj, they are not permitted during it, as this period calls for heightened self-restraint and devotion. Transgression—committing morally or spiritually wrong actions—is prohibited at all times, including during Hajj. The verse reassures pilgrims that Allah is fully aware of any good they do, encouraging sincere effort in both outward behavior and inner intention. Pilgrims are advised to take practical provisions for their journey, but the verse emphasizes that the best provision is righteousness—conduct rooted in deep awareness and reverence for Allah. It concludes by urging people of understanding to remain mindful of Allah, underscoring that spiritual preparation is just as important as physical readiness when undertaking this sacred journey.

Verse 198

لَيْسَ عَلَيْكُمْ جُنَاحٌ أَن تَبْتَغُوا فَضْلًا مِّن رَّبِّكُمْ ۚ فَإِذَا أَفَضْتُم مِّنْ عَرَفَاتٍ فَاذْكُرُوا اللَّهَ عِندَ الْمَشْعَرِ الْحَرَامِ ۖ وَاذْكُرُوهُ كَمَا هَدَاكُمْ وَإِن كُنتُم مِّن قَبْلِهِ لَمِنَ الضَّالِّينَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Building on the rules and conduct of Hajj, this verse clarifies that it is permissible to seek Allah’s bounty during the pilgrimage—such as through trade or commercial activity. This addresses a previous concern some had about combining acts of worship with earning a livelihood during Hajj. Engaging in trade while on pilgrimage is not only allowed but can also be seen as beneficial, as it helps meet the needs of fellow pilgrims. If those who provide goods and services were prohibited from doing so, who would support the practical needs of the pilgrims? The verse then describes a key ritual: remembering Allah at al-Mash‘ar al-Haram (the Sacred Monument at Muzdalifah) after departing from ‘Arafat, as an act of acknowledging Allah’s guidance. It reminds believers of the misguidance they were in before being guided through Islam, prompting a deep sense of gratitude.

Verse 199

ثُمَّ أَفِيضُوا مِنْ حَيْثُ أَفَاضَ النَّاسُ وَاسْتَغْفِرُوا اللَّهَ ۚ إِنَّ اللَّهَ غَفُورٌ رَّحِيمٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse addresses a pre-Islamic custom in which a certain group believed they held special status during the pilgrimage, granting themselves the privilege of setting out from a different location than the rest. The verse abolishes this distinction, instructing all pilgrims to depart from the same place, thereby reinforcing the principle of equality before Allah—a core value in Islam. After this shared act of remembrance and movement, the verse calls upon pilgrims to seek Allah’s forgiveness. It serves as a reminder that Allah is abundantly Forgiving and Merciful. This highlights that, regardless of one’s past mistakes or shortcomings, Allah’s pardon is always within reach for those who sincerely turn to Him.

Verse 200

فَإِذَا قَضَيْتُم مَّنَاسِكَكُمْ فَاذْكُرُوا اللَّهَ كَذِكْرِكُمْ آبَاءَكُمْ أَوْ أَشَدَّ ذِكْرًا ۗ فَمِنَ النَّاسِ مَن يَقُولُ رَبَّنَا آتِنَا فِي الدُّنْيَا وَمَا لَهُ فِي الْآخِرَةِ مِنْ خَلَاقٍ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse marks a transition from detailing specific pilgrimage rituals to addressing the inner state and spiritual focus after those rites are completed. It instructs Muslims to remember Allah with deep and sincere devotion, in contrast to the pre-Islamic custom where people would boast about their ancestors after pilgrimage. This shift underscores the transformation Islam introduced: moving from glorifying worldly lineage and past achievements to engaging in heartfelt remembrance of Allah. The verse then highlights a certain type of person—one who, even in supplication, is solely concerned with gaining the pleasures of this worldly life. Their prayers are limited to material gains, with no aspiration toward what is eternal or spiritually uplifting. This narrow focus reveals a lack of deeper faith. Allah makes this point clear at the end of the verse: “And among the people is he who says, ‘Our Lord, give us in this world,’ and he will have no share in the Hereafter.” This warns that by confining one’s desires to the present life, one risks forfeiting any reward in the life to come. Importantly, this verse leads into the next, which provides a more balanced perspective.

Verse 201

وَمِنْهُم مَّن يَقُولُ رَبَّنَا آتِنَا فِي الدُّنْيَا حَسَنَةً وَفِي الْآخِرَةِ حَسَنَةً وَقِنَا عَذَابَ النَّارِ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Following the description of those who seek only worldly gain, this verse presents a contrasting type of person. These are individuals whose prayers and aspirations are more balanced and comprehensive. They seek goodness not just in this life, but also in the Hereafter. This prayer acknowledges the importance of wellbeing in the present world while maintaining a strong connection to the eternal life and seeking protection from punishment in the afterlife. This demonstrates a broader vision and deeper understanding of existence and its purpose.

Verse 202

أُولَـٰئِكَ لَهُمْ نَصِيبٌ مِّمَّا كَسَبُوا ۚ وَاللَّهُ سَرِيعُ الْحِسَابِ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse highlights the reward for those who adopt the balanced perspective described in the previous verse—those who seek what is good both in this world and in the Hereafter. It assures that such individuals will be granted their reward in both realms, reflecting Allah’s justice and generosity. The verse also describes Allah as “swift in account,” serving as a powerful reminder that the reckoning is inevitable and will be carried out with absolute fairness and precision. This encourages believers to live with awareness of Allah, and to strive sincerely for what is truly lasting and beneficial—in both their worldly life and their eternal one.

Verse 203

وَاذْكُرُوا اللَّهَ فِي أَيَّامٍ مَّعْدُودَاتٍ ۚ فَمَن تَعَجَّلَ فِي يَوْمَيْنِ فَلَا إِثْمَ عَلَيْهِ وَمَن تَأَخَّرَ فَلَا إِثْمَ عَلَيْهِ ۚ لِمَنِ اتَّقَىٰ ۗ وَاتَّقُوا اللَّهَ وَاعْلَمُوا أَنَّكُمْ إِلَيْهِ تُحْشَرُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Continuing the theme of remembering Allah after the main pilgrimage rites, this verse refers to specific days following the Day of Sacrifice (the 10th of Dhu’l-Hijjah), often spent in Mina for rituals like stoning the pillars. It instructs pilgrims to continue remembering Allah during these "Appointed Days." It provides flexibility regarding the duration of stay in Mina; a pilgrim can leave after two days or stay longer, and neither is wrong, provided that their actions are motivated by a conscious desire to do what is right and remain aware of Allah. The verse concludes with a powerful reminder to remain conscious of Allah and to remember that everyone will ultimately be gathered before Him. This serves as an overarching principle for all actions, emphasizing accountability and the return to the Creator.

Verse 204

وَمِنَ النَّاسِ مَن يُعْجِبُكَ قَوْلُهُ فِي الْحَيَاةِ الدُّنْيَا وَيُشْهِدُ اللَّهَ عَلَىٰ مَا فِي قَلْبِهِ وَهُوَ أَلَدُّ الْخِصَامِ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse introduces a type of person who is very appealing in their speech. They might sound sincere and even invoke Allah to affirm their inner thoughts or intentions. However, the verse reveals that despite their outward charm, they are in reality the most hostile of adversaries. This highlights the importance of looking beyond smooth words to understand a person's true nature and intentions.

Verse 205

وَإِذَا تَوَلَّىٰ سَعَىٰ فِي الْأَرْضِ لِيُفْسِدَ فِيهَا وَيُهْلِكَ الْحَرْثَ وَالنَّسْلَ ۗ وَاللَّهُ لَا يُحِبُّ الْفَسَادَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Following on from the description in the previous verse, this verse describes the actions of the deceptive person when they are not directly facing you or are in a position of influence. It explains that their hidden hostility manifests as actively seeking to spread corruption, harm, and destruction in society, impacting even basic life like agriculture and livestock. This is strongly condemned, as Allah does not approve of mischief or corruption.

Verse 206

وَإِذَا قِيلَ لَهُ اتَّقِ اللَّهَ أَخَذَتْهُ الْعِزَّةُ بِالْإِثْمِ ۚ فَحَسْبُهُ جَهَنَّمُ ۚ وَلَبِئْسَ الْمِهَادُ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse completes the portrait of the insincere, hostile person by describing their reaction when confronted with guidance or reminded to be mindful of Allah. Instead of accepting advice, their arrogance drives them deeper into sin and wrongdoing. As a consequence, their ultimate destination is Hell, described here as a terrible resting place, serving as a stern warning about the outcome of persistent arrogance and rejection of truth. This connects back to the previous verses by showing the defiant attitude that stems from their inner corruption and outward deception.

Verse 207

وَمِنَ النَّاسِ مَن يَشْرِي نَفْسَهُ ابْتِغَاءَ مَرْضَاتِ اللَّهِ ۗ وَاللَّهُ رَءُوفٌ بِالْعِبَادِ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: In stark contrast to the insincere or worldly-focused individuals mentioned in the preceding verses, this verse introduces a completely different kind of person—one who sells their very self in pursuit of Allah’s pleasure. This individual is entirely devoted, willing to make the ultimate sacrifice out of sincere love and submission to Allah. Their actions are not driven by personal gain, but by a deep desire to earn Allah’s approval. Allah describes Himself as compassionate and kind toward such servants, affirming His special care for those who live with sincerity and devotion. This verse powerfully illustrates the positive outcome of a life centered on faith, highlighting the noble character and reward of one who gives everything for the sake of Allah. It stands as a striking contrast to those who seek only worldly benefit, reminding believers of the honor and divine favor that come with true sincerity.

Verse 208

يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا ادْخُلُوا فِي السِّلْمِ كَافَّةً وَلَا تَتَّبِعُوا خُطُوَاتِ الشَّيْطَانِ ۚ إِنَّهُ لَكُمْ عَدُوٌّ مُّبِينٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: After presenting those who respond to the divine call with disbelief or hypocrisy—and contrasting them with those who receive it with sincere faith—this verse turns directly to the believers. It calls them to enter into full and wholehearted submission to Allah. Islam is not meant to be followed selectively; rather, it must be embraced completely—in every dimension of life: spiritual, moral, social, and personal. The verse then issues a serious warning: do not follow the footsteps of Satan, for he is a clear and declared enemy. This highlights the sharp contrast between two paths: Allah’s path of guidance and Satan’s path of deception. Believers are urged to remain firm and unwavering in their commitment to Allah’s way, resisting all forms of distraction, compromise, and temptation that lead away from truth.

Verse 209

فَإِن زَلَلْتُم مِّن بَعْدِ مَا جَاءَتْكُمُ الْبَيِّنَاتُ فَاعْلَمُوا أَنَّ اللَّهَ عَزِيزٌ حَكِيمٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse follows the command for complete submission by warning of the consequences of turning away after receiving clear guidance. It reminds believers that once the truth has been made evident through signs and revelation, knowingly deviating from it is a serious matter. The verse affirms that Allah is Mighty and Wise, indicating that His justice is unchallengeable and His decrees are always based on perfect knowledge and wisdom.

Verse 210

هَلْ يَنظُرُونَ إِلَّا أَن يَأْتِيَهُمُ اللَّهُ فِي ظُلَلٍ مِّنَ الْغَمَامِ وَالْمَلَائِكَةُ وَقُضِيَ الْأَمْرُ ۚ وَإِلَى اللَّهِ تُرْجَعُ الْأُمُورُ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse challenges the hesitation of those who refuse to submit fully, asking rhetorically: are they waiting until Allah Himself comes to them with the angels before they finally believe? It points to the absurdity of waiting for a dramatic, undeniable manifestation of truth—because when that happens, the time for choice will have ended. That moment would be the Day of Judgment, when all veils are lifted, reality is laid bare, and no further repentance or belief will be accepted. The testing period of this worldly life will be over. Everything will be decisively settled, and all matters will return to Allah for final judgment.

Verse 211

سَلْ بَنِي إِسْرَائِيلَ كَمْ آتَيْنَاهُم مِّنْ آيَةٍ بَيِّنَةٍ ۗ وَمَن يُبَدِّلْ نِعْمَةَ اللَّهِ مِن بَعْدِ مَا جَاءَتْهُ فَإِنَّ اللَّهَ شَدِيدُ الْعِقَابِ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse draws attention to the historical example of the Children of Israel, who were recipients of divine revelation and numerous clear signs from Allah. Despite being favored with guidance, many among them either altered, ignored, or turned away from these blessings. The verse can be interpreted as a cautionary reminder for the current believing community, not to repeat the errors of those who came before them. Receiving Allah’s guidance is a great responsibility, and turning away from it after recognizing the truth is a serious offense. It ends with a sobering reminder that Allah is severe in punishment—especially for those who knowingly reject or distort His favor after it has been granted. This underlines the importance of preserving divine guidance with sincerity and integrity.

Verse 212

زُيِّنَ لِلَّذِينَ كَفَرُوا الْحَيَاةُ الدُّنْيَا وَيَسْخَرُونَ مِنَ الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا ۘ وَالَّذِينَ اتَّقَوْا فَوْقَهُمْ يَوْمَ الْقِيَامَةِ ۗ وَاللَّهُ يَرْزُقُ مَن يَشَاءُ بِغَيْرِ حِسَابٍ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse draws a striking contrast between those who reject faith and those who believe. It highlights how the comforts and pleasures of worldly life appear attractive to disbelievers, blinding them to deeper truths and leading them to ridicule those who live with faith and purpose. Their focus is limited to the material and temporary, while believers are anchored in values that transcend this life. The verse reassures believers that on the Day of Resurrection, the situation will be reversed: those who were mindful of Allah and lived righteously will be in a far higher position. It reminds believers that their worth is not determined by worldly status but by their relationship with Allah. Ultimately, the verse affirms that Allah provides abundantly to whom He wills, in this life and the next, in ways beyond human comprehension—underscoring that true success lies in divine favor, not worldly gain.

Verse 213

كَانَ النَّاسُ أُمَّةً وَاحِدَةً فَبَعَثَ اللَّهُ النَّبِيِّينَ مُبَشِّرِينَ وَمُنذِرِينَ وَأَنزَلَ مَعَهُمُ الْكِتَابَ بِالْحَقِّ لِيَحْكُمَ بَيْنَ النَّاسِ فِيمَا اخْتَلَفُوا فِيهِ ۚ وَمَا اخْتَلَفَ فِيهِ إِلَّا الَّذِينَ أُوتُوهُ مِن بَعْدِ مَا جَاءَتْهُمُ الْبَيِّنَاتُ بَغْيًا بَيْنَهُمْ ۖ فَهَدَى اللَّهُ الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا لِمَا اخْتَلَفُوا فِيهِ مِنَ الْحَقِّ بِإِذْنِهِ ۗ وَاللَّهُ يَهْدِي مَن يَشَاءُ إِلَىٰ صِرَاطٍ مُّسْتَقِيمٍ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse describes the origin of human society and its relationship with divine guidance. It begins by stating that humanity was once a single, united community. Allah revealed the truth from the very beginning to the first human, guiding them toward the right way of life. For a time, humanity remained on this path in harmony. However, as people spread and societies developed, disagreements and divisions emerged—not because the truth was hidden, but due to pride, ambition, and the desire for unjust gain. In response, Allah sent messengers to bring glad tidings and warnings. With them came divine revelation—the Book—as the ultimate reference to judge between people in their disputes. Despite this clear guidance, those who received the Book (such as the People of the Book) continued to differ, often driven by envy, selfishness, or the pursuit of power. Yet, through His grace, Allah guided the believers to the truth regarding the matters that were disputed. The verse ends by affirming that Allah guides whom He wills to the straight path, underscoring both human responsibility and divine mercy.

Verse 214

أَمْ حَسِبْتُمْ أَن تَدْخُلُوا الْجَنَّةَ وَلَمَّا يَأْتِكُم مَّثَلُ الَّذِينَ خَلَوْا مِن قَبْلِكُم ۖ مَّسَّتْهُمُ الْبَأْسَاءُ وَالضَّرَّاءُ وَزُلْزِلُوا حَتَّىٰ يَقُولَ الرَّسُولُ وَالَّذِينَ آمَنُوا مَعَهُ مَتَىٰ نَصْرُ اللَّهِ ۗ أَلَا إِنَّ نَصْرَ اللَّهِ قَرِيبٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse directly addresses believers, posing a profound question: Do you expect to enter Paradise without facing the kinds of trials and hardships endured by those who believed before you? It describes these past challenges as suffering and adversity so intense that they severely tested the spirit, leading even the Prophet at that time, and the believers with him, to question when Allah's help would arrive. The verse then immediately offers reassurance, declaring that the help of Allah is indeed always near. This highlights that enduring difficult trials and remaining steadfast is a necessary experience to purify and strengthen believers, preparing them for Allah's support and ultimately for the reward of Paradise. It serves as a timeless reminder that the path of faith often involves struggle, and perseverance through these difficulties is key to earning Allah's promise of assistance and ultimate success.

Verse 215

يَسْأَلُونَكَ مَاذَا يُنفِقُونَ ۖ قُلْ مَا أَنفَقْتُم مِّنْ خَيْرٍ فَلِلْوَالِدَيْنِ وَالْأَقْرَبِينَ وَالْيَتَامَىٰ وَالْمَسَاكِينِ وَابْنِ السَّبِيلِ ۗ وَمَا تَفْعَلُوا مِنْ خَيْرٍ فَإِنَّ اللَّهَ بِهِ عَلِيمٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse responds to a question from early Muslims about what they should spend in charity. It teaches that whatever good is given from one’s wealth should begin with those closest: parents and near relatives, followed by orphans, the needy, and the stranded traveler. This order emphasizes both personal responsibility and broader social concern. The verse encourages generosity that begins at home but extends outward, fostering compassion within families and strengthening society at large. It concludes by reminding us that Allah is fully aware of all the good deeds we do, including what we give in charity and the intentions behind those acts. This implies that our charitable actions—and the motives behind them—are known and will be rewarded. This verse sets the stage for further guidance by addressing specific questions raised by the believers, in the following verses.

Verse 216

كُتِبَ عَلَيْكُمُ الْقِتَالُ وَهُوَ كُرْهٌ لَّكُمْ ۖ وَعَسَىٰ أَن تَكْرَهُوا شَيْئًا وَهُوَ خَيْرٌ لَّكُمْ ۖ وَعَسَىٰ أَن تُحِبُّوا شَيْئًا وَهُوَ شَرٌّ لَّكُمْ ۗ وَاللَّهُ يَعْلَمُ وَأَنتُمْ لَا تَعْلَمُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Following the discussion on charity, this verse states a profound principle: something disliked may, in reality, be beneficial, while something beloved may prove harmful. In the specific context of fighting for a just cause, the verse acknowledges its difficulty but emphasizes its necessity for protecting the community and upholding justice. The message extends beyond the realm of warfare, applying broadly to life's many situations. Human perception is limited, while Allah's knowledge encompasses all outcomes. What appears unfavorable may hold hidden good, and what seems desirable may conceal harm. The verse, therefore, calls for trust in Allah’s wisdom, even when His guidance challenges personal inclinations or comfort.

Verse 217

يَسْأَلُونَكَ عَنِ الشَّهْرِ الْحَرَامِ قِتَالٍ فِيهِ ۖ قُلْ قِتَالٌ فِيهِ كَبِيرٌ ۖ وَصَدٌّ عَن سَبِيلِ اللَّهِ وَكُفْرٌ بِهِ وَالْمَسْجِدِ الْحَرَامِ وَإِخْرَاجُ أَهْلِهِ مِنْهُ أَكْبَرُ عِندَ اللَّهِ ۚ وَالْفِتْنَةُ أَكْبَرُ مِنَ الْقَتْلِ ۗ وَلَا يَزَالُونَ يُقَاتِلُونَكُمْ حَتَّىٰ يَرُدُّوكُمْ عَن دِينِكُمْ إِنِ اسْتَطَاعُوا ۚ وَمَن يَرْتَدِدْ مِنكُمْ عَن دِينِهِ فَيَمُتْ وَهُوَ كَافِرٌ فَأُولَـٰئِكَ حَبِطَتْ أَعْمَالُهُمْ فِي الدُّنْيَا وَالْآخِرَةِ ۖ وَأُولَـٰئِكَ أَصْحَابُ النَّارِ ۖ هُمْ فِيهَا خَالِدُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse directly answers a question about fighting during the sacred months, a period traditionally held as inviolable. It states clearly that fighting during this time is a serious matter. However, it immediately highlights that other actions are even more serious in the sight of Allah: preventing people from following Allah's path, rejecting faith in Him, obstructing access to the Sacred Mosque (in Mecca), and driving people out of their homes. The verse points out that oppression and persecution are worse than physical fighting or killing. It reveals the ultimate goal of those who oppose the Muslims: to make them abandon their faith. This clarifies that the aggression was initiated by the opponents, not the Muslims, and that the gravity of their actions – religious persecution and obstruction – far outweighs the specific incident of fighting in a sacred month. The verse then sternly warns that anyone who abandons their faith and dies as a disbeliever will lose everything in this life and the hereafter, facing severe consequences. This emphasizes the importance of holding firm to one's faith despite pressure. The subsequent verse offers hope to those who remain steadfast and strive for Allah's cause.

Verse 218

إِنَّ الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا وَالَّذِينَ هَاجَرُوا وَجَاهَدُوا فِي سَبِيلِ اللَّهِ أُولَـٰئِكَ يَرْجُونَ رَحْمَتَ اللَّهِ ۚ وَاللَّهُ غَفُورٌ رَّحِيمٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Following the warning in the previous verse about the consequences of abandoning faith under duress, this verse offers comfort and hope. It speaks of those who believed, who were forced to leave their homes (exiled), and who struggled and strove for the sake of Allah. These individuals, who faced immense hardship and persecution for their faith, are assured that they have hope of receiving Allah's mercy. It highlights the great value placed on sincere belief, sacrifice, and striving in the face of opposition. By mentioning Allah's attributes as Oft-Forgiving and Most Merciful, the verse reassures believers that their efforts and struggles are recognized and that Allah is ready to forgive any shortcomings and bestow His abundant mercy upon them. This serves as encouragement for believers to persevere.

Verse 219

يَسْأَلُونَكَ عَنِ الْخَمْرِ وَالْمَيْسِرِ ۖ قُلْ فِيهِمَا إِثْمٌ كَبِيرٌ وَمَنَافِعُ لِلنَّاسِ وَإِثْمُهُمَا أَكْبَرُ مِن نَّفْعِهِمَا ۗ وَيَسْأَلُونَكَ مَاذَا يُنفِقُونَ قُلِ الْعَفْوَ ۗ كَذَٰلِكَ يُبَيِّنُ اللَّهُ لَكُمُ الْآيَاتِ لَعَلَّكُمْ تَتَفَكَّرُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse addresses two separate questions. First, it tackles questions about intoxicants (like wine) and gambling, which were common practices at the time. The response acknowledges that while these may offer some temporary benefits or pleasures, their harm and negative consequences ("great sin") are far greater than any perceived profit. This highlights a principle for evaluating matters: weigh the good against the bad, and if the bad predominates, it should be avoided. This was an initial step in a gradual process of discouraging and eventually prohibiting these practices, recognizing they were deeply ingrained social habits. The second question in this verse asks about how much to spend in charity, building on the theme from verse 215. The answer is to spend "what is beyond your needs," meaning what is surplus to one's basic, reasonable requirements and those of one's dependents. This encourages generosity beyond the minimum obligation. The verse concludes by stating that Allah makes His signs and guidance clear in this way so that we may reflect upon our lives. This reflection is further elaborated in the next verse.

Verse 220

فِي الدُّنْيَا وَالْآخِرَةِ ۗ وَيَسْأَلُونَكَ عَنِ الْيَتَامَىٰ ۖ قُلْ إِصْلَاحٌ لَّهُمْ خَيْرٌ ۖ وَإِن تُخَالِطُوهُمْ فَإِخْوَانُكُمْ ۚ وَاللَّهُ يَعْلَمُ الْمُفْسِدَ مِنَ الْمُصْلِحِ ۚ وَلَوْ شَاءَ اللَّهُ لَأَعْنَتَكُمْ ۚ إِنَّ اللَّهَ عَزِيزٌ حَكِيمٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse continues the reflection mentioned at the end of verse 219, urging us to consider the implications of Allah's guidance on both this life and the life to come. It then addresses a new question concerning the treatment of orphans. It instructs that the guiding principle for those caring for orphans should be to do what is best for their welfare and improvement. If guardians find it easier or more practical to mix the orphans' affairs or property with their own, they are reminded that the orphans are their brothers and sisters in faith, implying a responsibility based on kinship and brotherhood. However, it sternly warns against exploiting or harming the orphans' interests under the guise of mixing affairs. Allah, who has perfect knowledge, can distinguish between those who genuinely intend good for the orphans and those who intend harm or mischief. The verse concludes by emphasizing that Allah, in His power and wisdom, could have made matters more difficult for us, but He has provided clear guidance out of His mercy. This serves as a reminder of Allah's grace and the ease He intends for believers, encouraging them to act sincerely according to His guidance, especially concerning the vulnerable.

Verse 221

وَلَا تَنكِحُوا الْمُشْرِكَاتِ حَتَّىٰ يُؤْمِنَّ ۚ وَلَأَمَةٌ مُّؤْمِنَةٌ خَيْرٌ مِّن مُّشْرِكَةٍ وَلَوْ أَعْجَبَتْكُمْ ۗ وَلَا تُنكِحُوا الْمُشْرِكِينَ حَتَّىٰ يُؤْمِنُوا ۚ وَلَعَبْدٌ مُّؤْمِنٌ خَيْرٌ مِّن مُّشْرِكٍ وَلَوْ أَعْجَبَكُمْ ۗ أُولَـٰئِكَ يَدْعُونَ إِلَى النَّارِ ۖ وَاللَّهُ يَدْعُو إِلَى الْجَنَّةِ وَالْمَغْفِرَةِ بِإِذْنِهِ ۖ وَيُبَيِّنُ آيَاتِهِ لِلنَّاسِ لَعَلَّهُمْ يَتَذَكَّرُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse addresses the foundational relationship of marriage within the Muslim community. It teaches that Muslims should not marry individuals who associate partners with Allah—a practice known as shirk, commonly referring to polytheists or idolaters—unless they come to believe in the One God. Even a believing slave woman, despite her lower social status, is considered better than an idolatrous woman, regardless of her physical attractiveness. This is because the spiritual connection built on shared faith is deeper and more enduring than physical appeal or social rank. Similarly, Muslim women are instructed not to marry men who commit shirk unless they embrace belief. The verse explains that those who reject faith lead others toward misguidance and ultimately the Fire, while Allah calls people toward His mercy, forgiveness, and Paradise. By encouraging marriages built on shared belief, the verse helps preserve the spiritual direction and identity of the Muslim community.

Verse 222

وَيَسْأَلُونَكَ عَنِ الْمَحِيضِ ۖ قُلْ هُوَ أَذًى فَاعْتَزِلُوا النِّسَاءَ فِي الْمَحِيضِ ۖ وَلَا تَقْرَبُوهُنَّ حَتَّىٰ يَطْهُرْنَ ۖ فَإِذَا تَطَهَّرْنَ فَأْتُوهُنَّ مِنْ حَيْثُ أَمَرَكُمُ اللَّهُ ۚ إِنَّ اللَّهَ يُحِبُّ التَّوَّابِينَ وَيُحِبُّ الْمُتَطَهِّرِينَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Following the guidelines on who Muslims should marry, this verse addresses matters of physical intimacy within marriage, specifically regarding menstruation. When asked about this, the answer given is that it is an unclean condition. Therefore, men are instructed to keep away from sexual intimacy with their wives during their menstrual periods and not to approach them in that way until they are clean. Once the women have purified themselves (after menstruation), couples may resume physical intimacy in the proper way as ordained by Allah. By following these guidelines and maintaining purity, Muslims demonstrate obedience to Allah, for He loves those who turn to Him in repentance and those who keep themselves pure. This verse transitions the discussion from the selection of a marriage partner to the purity and conduct within the marital relationship itself.

Verse 223

نِسَاؤُكُمْ حَرْثٌ لَّكُمْ فَأْتُوا حَرْثَكُمْ أَنَّىٰ شِئْتُمْ ۖ وَقَدِّمُوا لِأَنفُسِكُمْ ۚ وَاتَّقُوا اللَّهَ وَاعْلَمُوا أَنَّكُم مُّلَاقُوهُ ۗ وَبَشِّرِ الْمُؤْمِنِينَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse continues the discussion of marital intimacy, following the previous guidance on purity after menstruation. It uses the metaphor of wives as a "tilth"—fertile land—to illustrate the role of marriage in procreation and nurturing future generations. The imagery emphasizes both the physical and generative aspects of intimacy, within a lawful and respectful marital bond. Husbands are told they may approach their wives in the manner they desire, which scholars interpret as allowing flexibility in intimacy, provided it remains within the bounds of mutual respect and dignity. However, this physical relationship is not detached from spiritual responsibility. The verse advises believers to “send forth something good for yourselves,” a phrase open to multiple layers of meaning. One interpretation is that people should ensure the continuation of the human race. Another is that they should strive to raise a righteous generation—children with faith, morality, and humanity. The verse concludes by urging believers to remain mindful of Allah, and to remember that they will ultimately meet Him. This connects the physical dimension of marriage to spiritual awareness and accountability in the Hereafter. It reminds believers that even the intimate parts of life are part of a divinely guided path, contributing to both personal and communal well-being. In this way, marital intimacy is integrated into the broader framework of taqwa (God-consciousness) and worship.

Verse 224

وَلَا تَجْعَلُوا اللَّهَ عُرْضَةً لِّأَيْمَانِكُمْ أَن تَبَرُّوا وَتَتَّقُوا وَتُصْلِحُوا بَيْنَ النَّاسِ ۗ وَاللَّهُ سَمِيعٌ عَلِيمٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse shifts the focus to the subject of oaths, particularly those sworn in the name of Allah. It teaches that invoking Allah's name should not be used as a pretext to avoid doing what is good, acting with righteousness, or making peace between people. If someone takes an oath that would prevent them from carrying out a beneficial or moral act, it is better to break that oath and do the good deed—then make the prescribed kaffārah (atonement) for the broken oath, as outlined in Islamic law. The verse establishes a key principle: the promotion of goodness, justice, and reconciliation takes precedence over rigidly sticking to an oath that leads to harm or neglect of moral duty. This also reminds believers that Allah is All-Hearing and All-Knowing, emphasizing the seriousness of invoking His name and the need to act with sincerity and accountability. This verse serves as a general guideline on the ethics of oaths before proceeding to specific rulings related to marital relationships in the following verses.

Verse 225

لَّا يُؤَاخِذُكُمُ اللَّهُ بِاللَّغْوِ فِي أَيْمَانِكُمْ وَلَـٰكِن يُؤَاخِذُكُم بِمَا كَسَبَتْ قُلُوبُكُمْ ۗ وَاللَّهُ غَفُورٌ حَلِيمٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Following the general principle about oaths in the previous verse, this verse offers further clarification about accountability. It teaches that Allah does not hold people accountable for oaths made thoughtlessly or spoken out of habit, where there was no firm or deliberate intention behind the words. These casual or reflexive expressions—common in everyday speech—are not treated as binding. However, when an oath is made with conscious intent and true commitment in the heart, a person becomes morally and spiritually accountable for it. This emphasizes the central Islamic principle that intent (niyyah) is a key factor in determining responsibility and judgment. The verse ends by highlighting two of Allah’s names: Al-Ghafūr (the Most Forgiving) and Al-Ḥalīm (the Most Forbearing), underscoring His mercy and leniency towards His servants. These attributes reassure believers that Allah pardons unintentional mistakes and provides a path for redemption through atonement when needed. This verse serves as both a warning about the weight of intentional speech and a comfort in Allah’s compassion.

Verse 226

لِّلَّذِينَ يُؤْلُونَ مِن نِّسَائِهِمْ تَرَبُّصُ أَرْبَعَةِ أَشْهُرٍ ۖ فَإِن فَاءُوا فَإِنَّ اللَّهَ غَفُورٌ رَّحِيمٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse addresses a specific type of oath in the context of marriage: when a husband swears to abstain from sexual relations with his wife. In pre-Islamic times, such oaths—known as īlāʾ—were sometimes used as a means of emotional punishment or control, leaving the wife in a state of uncertainty and neglect. Islam places firm boundaries on this practice by establishing a maximum waiting period of four months. This period serves as a cooling-off phase and a test of the relationship. If during or after these four months the husband chooses to resume marital relations, it is allowed and even encouraged. The verse assures that Allah is Oft-Forgiving and Most Merciful, signaling that returning to marital intimacy after such a break is not sinful but can be a step toward reconciliation. This ruling ensures fairness and protection for the wife, preventing prolonged emotional harm or abandonment. It reflects Islam’s emphasis on responsibility, compassion, and the maintenance of family harmony, while limiting harmful practices inherited from earlier traditions.

Verse 227

وَإِنْ عَزَمُوا الطَّلَاقَ فَإِنَّ اللَّهَ سَمِيعٌ عَلِيمٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse continues the legal and ethical guidance from the previous verse concerning the īlāʾ—the oath of abstention in marriage. It specifies that if, after the four-month waiting period mentioned in verse 2:226, the husband does not resume marital relations and instead intends to end the marriage, then this intention constitutes a formal decision for divorce. The verse confirms that such a decision leads to the dissolution of the marriage, making it clear that a husband cannot leave his wife in limbo indefinitely. The verse concludes by emphasizing that Allah is All-Hearing and All-Knowing, reminding believers that Allah is fully aware of both actions and intentions. This guidance ensures the protection of the wife's rights, prevents emotional harm through prolonged uncertainty, and upholds justice and clarity in marital relationships. It reinforces Islam’s concern for fairness and accountability in family life.

Verse 228

وَالْمُطَلَّقَاتُ يَتَرَبَّصْنَ بِأَنفُسِهِنَّ ثَلَاثَةَ قُرُوءٍ ۚ وَلَا يَحِلُّ لَهُنَّ أَن يَكْتُمْنَ مَا خَلَقَ اللَّهُ فِي أَرْحَامِهِنَّ إِن كُنَّ يُؤْمِنَّ بِاللَّهِ وَالْيَوْمِ الْآخِرِ ۚ وَبُعُولَتُهُنَّ أَحَقُّ بِرَدِّهِنَّ فِي ذَٰلِكَ إِنْ أَرَادُوا إِصْلَاحًا ۚ وَلَهُنَّ مِثْلُ الَّذِي عَلَيْهِنَّ بِالْمَعْرُوفِ ۚ وَلِلرِّجَالِ عَلَيْهِنَّ دَرَجَةٌ ۗ وَاللَّهُ عَزِيزٌ حَكِيمٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse introduces foundational rulings concerning divorce in Islam, beginning with the `iddah—the waiting period a divorced woman must observe. It stipulates that a woman who is divorced must wait for three menstrual cycles (or periods of purity, according to some jurists) before she can remarry. This waiting period serves multiple purposes: it allows for the detection of a possible pregnancy, ensures clarity regarding lineage, and provides a window for reflection and potential reconciliation. During this time, it is strictly prohibited for a woman to conceal a pregnancy, should one exist. This command is reinforced by appealing to her belief in Allah and the Last Day, highlighting the moral and spiritual seriousness of such concealment. The verse also grants the husband the right to take his wife back during this waiting period if his intention is genuine reconciliation. In this case, no new marriage contract is required. This right is restricted to the initial two divorces and must not be used as a means of manipulation. Importantly, the verse states that women have rights comparable to the obligations placed upon them, in a just and balanced manner. However, it acknowledges that men have a "degree" over women in certain aspects—such as initiating divorce and the legal authority to resume the marriage during the `iddah. This "degree" is not a blanket superiority, but rather a functional distinction within the structure of Islamic family law. The verse ends by affirming that Allah is Exalted in Might and Wise, underscoring that these rulings are grounded in divine justice and wisdom.

Verse 229

الطَّلَاقُ مَرَّتَانِ ۖ فَإِمْسَاكٌ بِمَعْرُوفٍ أَوْ تَسْرِيحٌ بِإِحْسَانٍ ۗ وَلَا يَحِلُّ لَكُمْ أَن تَأْخُذُوا مِمَّا آتَيْتُمُوهُنَّ شَيْئًا إِلَّا أَن يَخَافَا أَلَّا يُقِيمَا حُدُودَ اللَّهِ ۖ فَإِنْ خِفْتُمْ أَلَّا يُقِيمَا حُدُودَ اللَّهِ فَلَا جُنَاحَ عَلَيْهِمَا فِيمَا افْتَدَتْ بِهِ ۗ تِلْكَ حُدُودُ اللَّهِ فَلَا تَعْتَدُوهَا ۚ وَمَن يَتَعَدَّ حُدُودَ اللَّهِ فَأُولَـٰئِكَ هُمُ الظَّالِمُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse explains how divorce works in Islam and sets clear boundaries to prevent its misuse. It allows a husband to divorce and take back his wife two times. However, if he divorces her a third time, the separation becomes final, and they cannot remarry unless certain conditions are met. The purpose of this rule is to protect both spouses, especially women, from emotional harm. Before Islam came, some men would repeatedly divorce and take back their wives out of anger, without any real intention to maintain a respectful and lasting relationship. This verse puts an end to that by limiting how often divorce can be reversed. It also teaches fairness when parting ways. A husband should not take back any gifts or dowry he gave his wife, even after divorce. Islam encourages kindness and respect, especially during difficult times like separation. There’s also a special case called khul’, where a woman can request a divorce if she feels the marriage cannot continue peacefully. In this case, she may offer compensation to the husband, like returning part of the dowry. This ensures that women also have a way out of unhappy marriages, while keeping the process fair for both sides. The verse ends by reminding people to respect these boundaries, as they are meant to ensure justice, dignity, and stability in family life.

Verse 230

فَإِن طَلَّقَهَا فَلَا تَحِلُّ لَهُ مِن بَعْدُ حَتَّىٰ تَنكِحَ زَوْجًا غَيْرَهُ ۗ فَإِن طَلَّقَهَا فَلَا جُنَاحَ عَلَيْهِمَا أَن يَتَرَاجَعَا إِن ظَنَّا أَن يُقِيمَا حُدُودَ اللَّهِ ۗ وَتِلْكَ حُدُودُ اللَّهِ يُبَيِّنُهَا لِقَوْمٍ يَعْلَمُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse explains what happens after a third divorce. If a husband divorces his wife for the third time, the separation becomes final and cannot be reversed. They are not allowed to remarry immediately. For the couple to marry again, the wife must first marry another man, and that second marriage must end naturally—either through divorce or the husband's passing. Only after this, and after completing her waiting period (iddah), can she remarry her first husband. This is only allowed if both sincerely believe they can now respect the boundaries set by Allah and live together in a healthy, committed way. This strict condition serves as a serious warning. It encourages couples to think carefully before choosing divorce, especially a second or third time. It helps prevent using divorce lightly or as a means of manipulation. The verse ends by reminding us that these are Allah’s clear boundaries, given for those who are willing to think and understand. It emphasizes the wisdom behind these limits and their role in protecting the dignity and stability of marriage.

Verse 231

وَإِذَا طَلَّقْتُمُ النِّسَاءَ فَبَلَغْنَ أَجَلَهُنَّ فَأَمْسِكُوهُنَّ بِمَعْرُوفٍ أَوْ سَرِّحُوهُنَّ بِمَعْرُوفٍ ۚ وَلَا تُمْسِكُوهُنَّ ضِرَارًا لِّتَعْتَدُوا ۚ وَمَن يَفْعَلْ ذَٰلِكَ فَقَدْ ظَلَمَ نَفْسَهُ ۚ وَلَا تَتَّخِذُوا آيَاتِ اللَّهِ هُزُوًا ۚ وَاذْكُرُوا نِعْمَتَ اللَّهِ عَلَيْكُمْ وَمَا أَنزَلَ عَلَيْكُم مِّنَ الْكِتَابِ وَالْحِكْمَةِ يَعِظُكُم بِهِ ۚ وَاتَّقُوا اللَّهَ وَاعْلَمُوا أَنَّ اللَّهَ بِكُلِّ شَيْءٍ عَلِيمٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse guides what should happen near the end of the waiting period (iddah) after a divorce. It tells husbands that when this period is almost over, they must make a sincere choice: either take their wife back into the marriage in a fair and respectful way, or let her go with kindness and dignity. This verse deals with a specific situation—when a man has divorced his wife, and her waiting period is almost complete. He still has time to either reconcile or part ways peacefully. The following verse (2:232) addresses what happens after the waiting period ends. Importantly, this verse strictly forbids taking the wife back just to cause harm or to manipulate her. Such behavior is unjust and considered a wrongdoing against oneself, as it goes against the guidance of Allah. The verse also gives a strong reminder: do not treat the commands of Allah lightly. It urges believers to be thankful for the blessings Allah has given them—especially the Qur’an and the wisdom it contains. These are meant to guide people toward fairness and integrity. It ends by reminding everyone that Allah is fully aware of all actions, encouraging sincerity, justice, and mindfulness in handling sensitive matters. This connects directly to the earlier verses about divorce, reinforcing that the process must be completed with honesty and respect.

Verse 232

وَإِذَا طَلَّقْتُمُ النِّسَاءَ فَبَلَغْنَ أَجَلَهُنَّ فَلَا تَعْضُلُوهُنَّ أَن يَنكِحْنَ أَزْوَاجَهُنَّ إِذَا تَرَاضَوْا بَيْنَهُم بِالْمَعْرُوفِ ۗ ذَٰلِكَ يُوعَظُ بِهِ مَن كَانَ مِنكُمْ يُؤْمِنُ بِاللَّهِ وَالْيَوْمِ الْآخِرِ ۗ ذَٰلِكُمْ أَزْكَىٰ لَكُمْ وَأَطْهَرُ ۗ وَاللَّهُ يَعْلَمُ وَأَنتُمْ لَا تَعْلَمُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse follows the previous guidance about the end of the waiting period (iddah) after divorce. It shifts focus to the woman’s family or guardians. If a divorced woman and her former husband mutually wish to remarry on respectful and honorable terms, her guardians are instructed not to prevent it. This command is directed toward those who truly believe in Allah and the Last Day, reminding them that supporting such a decision reflects sincere faith. The verse highlights that allowing the couple to reunite is the most upright and pure choice. It ends by affirming that Allah knows best, even when people may not fully understand the wisdom behind His guidance.

Verse 233

وَالْوَالِدَاتُ يُرْضِعْنَ أَوْلَادَهُنَّ حَوْلَيْنِ كَامِلَيْنِ ۖ لِمَنْ أَرَادَ أَن يُتِمَّ الرَّضَاعَةَ ۚ وَعَلَى الْمَوْلُودِ لَهُ رِزْقُهُنَّ وَكِسْوَتُهُنَّ بِالْمَعْرُوفِ ۚ لَا تُكَلَّفُ نَفْسٌ إِلَّا وُسْعَهَا ۚ لَا تُضَارَّ وَالِدَةٌ بِوَلَدِهَا وَلَا مَوْلُودٌ لَّهُ بِوَلَدِهِ ۚ وَعَلَى الْوَارِثِ مِثْلُ ذَٰلِكَ ۗ فَإِنْ أَرَادَا فِصَالًا عَن تَرَاضٍ مِّنْهُمَا وَتَشَاوُرٍ فَلَا جُنَاحَ عَلَيْهِمَا ۗ وَإِنْ أَرَدتُّمْ أَن تَسْتَرْضِعُوا أَوْلَادَكُمْ فَلَا جُنَاحَ عَلَيْكُمْ إِذَا سَلَّمْتُم مَّا آتَيْتُم بِالْمَعْرُوفِ ۗ وَاتَّقُوا اللَّهَ وَاعْلَمُوا أَنَّ اللَّهَ بِمَا تَعْمَلُونَ بَصِيرٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse addresses the responsibilities of parents after divorce, focusing on the care of young children—especially breastfeeding. It says that mothers may nurse their children for up to two full years if they choose to complete the full period of breastfeeding. During this time, the father is responsible for supporting the mother by providing her with food and clothing in a fair and appropriate way. The verse sets a general principle: no one should be burdened beyond their capacity, and neither parent should suffer unfairly because of their child. If the father has passed away, his legal heir must take over this responsibility. Parents are also allowed to make mutual decisions based on what is best for the child. If, after discussion, they agree to wean the child before two years, it is permitted. Likewise, hiring a wet nurse is allowed, as long as she is compensated fairly. This verse places the child's well-being at the center of all decisions and concludes with a reminder to remain mindful of Allah, who sees all that we do. It encourages fairness, cooperation, and integrity in parenting responsibilities.

Verse 234

وَالَّذِينَ يُتَوَفَّوْنَ مِنكُمْ وَيَذَرُونَ أَزْوَاجًا يَتَرَبَّصْنَ بِأَنفُسِهِنَّ أَرْبَعَةَ أَشْهُرٍ وَعَشْرًا ۖ فَإِذَا بَلَغْنَ أَجَلَهُنَّ فَلَا جُنَاحَ عَلَيْكُمْ فِيمَا فَعَلْنَ فِي أَنفُسِهِنَّ بِالْمَعْرُوفِ ۗ وَاللَّهُ بِمَا تَعْمَلُونَ خَبِيرٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Moving from divorce to the situation of widows, this verse establishes a waiting period for women whose husbands have passed away. They are to wait for four months and ten days. This waiting period serves multiple purposes, including ascertaining whether the widow is pregnant and providing a period of mourning and adjustment. Once this specific period is over, the verse states that there is no blame upon the guardians or community if the widow then makes decisions about her life, such as remarrying, in a just and appropriate manner according to Islamic teachings. This instruction helped abolish harsh pre-Islamic customs regarding widows. The verse ends by reminding everyone that Allah is fully aware of all actions, emphasizing accountability.

Verse 235

وَلَا جُنَاحَ عَلَيْكُمْ فِيمَا عَرَّضْتُم بِهِ مِنْ خِطْبَةِ النِّسَاءِ أَوْ أَكْنَنتُمْ فِي أَنفُسِكُمْ ۚ عَلِمَ اللَّهُ أَنَّكُمْ سَتَذْكُرُونَهُنَّ وَلَـٰكِن لَّا تُوَاعِدُوهُنَّ سِرًّا إِلَّا أَن تَقُولُوا قَوْلًا مَّعْرُوفًا ۚ وَلَا تَعْزِمُوا عُقْدَةَ النِّكَاحِ حَتَّىٰ يَبْلُغَ الْكِتَابُ أَجَلَهُ ۚ وَاعْلَمُوا أَنَّ اللَّهَ يَعْلَمُ مَا فِي أَنفُسِكُمْ فَاحْذَرُوهُ ۚ وَاعْلَمُوا أَنَّ اللَّهَ غَفُورٌ حَلِيمٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse continues addressing the situation of widows after the death of their husbands, specifically regarding marriage proposals. It states that there is no blame on someone who hints at a marriage offer after the widow’s waiting period has ended, or even if they merely keep such an intention in their heart during the waiting period. Allah is fully aware of what people think and feel. However, the verse strictly prohibits making a secret or binding marriage agreement during the waiting period or finalizing the marriage before that time is complete. Any interaction should be conducted respectfully and honorably. It emphasizes the need to remain mindful of Allah, who knows what is in people’s hearts, and reminds believers that Allah is Most Forgiving and Most Forbearing. The verse encourages adherence to these guidelines while recognizing human emotions.

Verse 236

لَّا جُنَاحَ عَلَيْكُمْ إِن طَلَّقْتُمُ النِّسَاءَ مَا لَمْ تَمَسُّوهُنَّ أَوْ تَفْرِضُوا لَهُنَّ فَرِيضَةً ۚ وَمَتِّعُوهُنَّ عَلَى الْمُوسِعِ قَدَرُهُ وَعَلَى الْمُقْتِرِ قَدَرُهُ مَتَاعًا بِالْمَعْرُوفِ ۖ حَقًّا عَلَى الْمُحْسِنِينَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse deals with a situation where a couple is formally married, but the marriage ends in divorce before they have been intimate, and before a dowry (mahr) has been fixed. In such a case, the Qur'an states that there is no sin on the man for initiating the divorce. However, to uphold dignity and kindness, he is still encouraged to offer a parting gift to the woman. The amount of this gift is not fixed; it depends on what he can afford—whether he is wealthy or has limited means. This gesture shows compassion and ensures that even in brief or incomplete marriages, the woman is not left without honor or support. It also encourages men to act with fairness, generosity, and moral integrity.

Verse 237

وَإِن طَلَّقْتُمُوهُنَّ مِن قَبْلِ أَن تَمَسُّوهُنَّ وَقَدْ فَرَضْتُمْ لَهُنَّ فَرِيضَةً فَنِصْفُ مَا فَرَضْتُمْ إِلَّا أَن يَعْفُونَ أَوْ يَعْفُوَ الَّذِي بِيَدِهِ عُقْدَةُ النِّكَاحِ ۚ وَأَن تَعْفُوا أَقْرَبُ لِلتَّقْوَىٰ ۚ وَلَا تَنسَوُا الْفَضْلَ بَيْنَكُمْ ۚ إِنَّ اللَّهَ بِمَا تَعْمَلُونَ بَصِيرٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse continues from the previous one, addressing the case where a couple is divorced before consummation, but a dowry (mahr) was already agreed upon. In such a case, the woman is entitled to half of the agreed amount. However, this can be adjusted through generosity—either the woman may choose to give up her share, or the one who holds authority over the marriage bond may choose to relinquish it. The verse praises such acts of kindness as closer to righteousness. It ends by encouraging both sides to act with grace and not forget generosity, even in separation, and reminds that Allah is fully aware of all actions.

Verse 238

حَافِظُوا عَلَى الصَّلَوَاتِ وَالصَّلَاةِ الْوُسْطَىٰ وَقُومُوا لِلَّهِ قَانِتِينَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse shifts focus from family matters to the fundamental act of worship in Islam: prayer. It commands believers to be diligent and consistent in observing their prayers, singling out the "Middle Prayer" for special emphasis (which Muslims understand refers to the 'Asr or mid-afternoon prayer, a time when people might be easily distracted). It instructs believers to stand before Allah in prayer with a state of devout obedience and humility. This interjection of prayer within verses about family law highlights that all aspects of a Muslim's life, including personal and social relations, are connected to the worship and remembrance of Allah, showing that adherence to these divine laws is itself an act of devotion.

Verse 239

فَإِنْ خِفْتُمْ فَرِجَالًا أَوْ رُكْبَانًا ۖ فَإِذَا أَمِنتُمْ فَاذْكُرُوا اللَّهَ كَمَا عَلَّمَكُم مَّا لَمْ تَكُونُوا تَعْلَمُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Continuing the theme of prayer from the previous verse, this verse offers guidance for performing prayer during times of fear or danger, such as when facing an enemy. It allows flexibility—permitting prayer while walking or riding, in any posture that is safe and practical under the circumstances, even if it deviates from the usual form. Once safety is restored, however, believers are instructed to return to performing prayer fully and properly, in the complete manner Allah has taught. This underscores the constant importance of prayer and remembrance of Allah in all conditions—from peace to peril—while also acknowledging human limitations and the mercy built into divine guidance.

Verse 240

وَالَّذِينَ يُتَوَفَّوْنَ مِنكُمْ وَيَذَرُونَ أَزْوَاجًا وَصِيَّةً لِّأَزْوَاجِهِم مَّتَاعًا إِلَى الْحَوْلِ غَيْرَ إِخْرَاجٍ ۚ فَإِنْ خَرَجْنَ فَلَا جُنَاحَ عَلَيْكُمْ فِي مَا فَعَلْنَ فِي أَنفُسِهِنَّ مِن مَّعْرُوفٍ ۗ وَاللَّهُ عَزِيزٌ حَكِيمٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse returns to the subject of widows and outlines a compassionate directive for husbands nearing death. It instructs such men to make a will that provides their wives with maintenance and residence for one year. This bequest offered widows the right to stay in their deceased husband's home without being evicted, supported financially for that time. However, if the widow chooses to leave the residence after completing her waiting period—four months and ten days as mandated in verse 2:234—then there is no blame on the family or community for what she lawfully chooses to do with her life.
