This verse establishes two layers of guidance. The first is obligatory: the widow must observe a waiting period of four months and ten days. The second is a recommended provision—staying for up to a year if the husband has bequeathed it. This one-year stay is not compulsory, and the widow is free to leave earlier if she wishes, as long as her choice is made freely and without pressure. The instruction reflects both compassion and flexibility in addressing the widow’s emotional and practical needs. It’s also important to note that this guidance applies when the home has not already been formally inherited by the widow under the inheritance laws described in Surah 4:12. If she has inherited the home or a share in it, then her right to residence would follow from that legal ownership. The verse ends by affirming that Allah is Exalted in Might and Wise, highlighting that these rulings are not arbitrary, but are based on divine wisdom and mercy. They aim to balance justice, emotional well-being, and practical support for those dealing with loss.

Verse 241

وَلِلْمُطَلَّقَاتِ مَتَاعٌ بِالْمَعْرُوفِ ۖ حَقًّا عَلَى الْمُتَّقِينَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse follows the detailed rulings on divorce. It emphasizes the right of divorced women to receive fair support or provisions from their former husbands. This isn't just about financial maintenance; it's intended to help ease the difficulties and emotional impact of separation. Providing this support justly is considered a duty, especially for those who strive to live righteously and are mindful of Allah. The provision might lessen the bitterness and sadness that can come with divorce, highlighting Islam's emphasis on kindness and fairness even when a marriage has ended.

Verse 242

كَذَٰلِكَ يُبَيِّنُ اللَّهُ لَكُمْ آيَاتِهِ لَعَلَّكُمْ تَعْقِلُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse serves as a concluding statement for the preceding set of rulings, including those on marriage, divorce, and related matters. It highlights that Allah makes His guidelines clear and understandable for humanity. The purpose behind making these rules clear is so that people can grasp their meaning and significance. It reminds us that Allah presents His laws clearly, fairly, and effectively, inviting us to reflect on their value and recognize His guidance and mercy in them. These teachings are meant to bring harmony and understanding to our relationships and society, emphasizing that following Allah's commands is beneficial for our lives, both individually and collectively.

Verse 243

أَلَمْ تَرَ إِلَى الَّذِينَ خَرَجُوا مِن دِيَارِهِمْ وَهُمْ أُلُوفٌ حَذَرَ الْمَوْتِ فَقَالَ لَهُمُ اللَّهُ مُوتُوا ثُمَّ أَحْيَاهُمْ ۚ إِنَّ اللَّهَ لَذُو فَضْلٍ عَلَى النَّاسِ وَلَـٰكِنَّ أَكْثَرَ النَّاسِ لَا يَشْكُرُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse invites us to reflect on a story from the past: a large group of people who left their homes in great numbers because they were afraid of dying. The Qur’an doesn’t specify exactly what they feared—it could have been a deadly disease or an enemy threat. That detail isn’t the focus. What matters is that they tried to escape death. Despite their efforts, they still died. The verse makes it clear: no one can escape what Allah has decreed. Then, in a powerful sign, Allah brought them back to life—so that they, and others after them, could learn a lesson. It shows that life and death are completely in Allah’s hands. Human efforts cannot stop what Allah has already destined. By leaving out names, places, and specific times, the Qur’an turns this story into a timeless message. It’s not limited to one group of people—it speaks to everyone. The verse ends by reminding us that Allah gives many blessings to humanity, but many people are still ungrateful.

Verse 244

وَقَاتِلُوا فِي سَبِيلِ اللَّهِ وَاعْلَمُوا أَنَّ اللَّهَ سَمِيعٌ عَلِيمٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Following the previous verse which showed Allah's absolute control over life and death, this verse directly addresses believers, encouraging them to strive and fight for the cause of Allah. It implies that since life and death are in Allah's hands anyway, the fear of death should not hold back someone who is committed to Allah's path. When striving for Allah's cause, believers are reminded that Allah hears their prayers and their intentions, and He knows all things, including their actions and their sincerity.

Verse 245

مَّن ذَا الَّذِي يُقْرِضُ اللَّهَ قَرْضًا حَسَنًا فَيُضَاعِفَهُ لَهُ أَضْعَافًا كَثِيرَةً ۚ وَاللَّهُ يَقْبِضُ وَيَبْسُطُ وَإِلَيْهِ تُرْجَعُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse continues the theme of striving and sacrifice from the previous verse, but now it focuses on financial giving for the sake of Allah. It asks a rhetorical question: "Who will give a good loan to Allah?" Of course, Allah does not need anything from us—so this is symbolic language. It refers to giving wealth with pure intentions, to support causes that are pleasing to Allah. The phrase “goodly loan” means giving selflessly, without expecting anything in return from people. It must be sincere, done purely for Allah’s sake—not for praise, recognition, or personal gain. The verse promises that Allah will repay such giving with multiplied rewards. In fact, Allah considers what a person gives in this way as if it were given directly to Him, and He guarantees to return it many times over. The verse also reminds us that wealth comes from Allah—whether in large or small amounts—and that eventually, everything we have, and we ourselves, will return to Him. This teaches that wealth is not something we truly own; it is a trust from Allah, meant to be used responsibly and generously in ways that earn His pleasure.

Verse 246

أَلَمْ تَرَ إِلَى الْمَلَإِ مِن بَنِي إِسْرَائِيلَ مِن بَعْدِ مُوسَىٰ إِذْ قَالُوا لِنَبِيٍّ لَّهُمُ ابْعَثْ لَنَا مَلِكًا نُّقَاتِلْ فِي سَبِيلِ اللَّهِ ۖ قَالَ هَلْ عَسَيْتُمْ إِن كُتِبَ عَلَيْكُمُ الْقِتَالُ أَلَّا تُقَاتِلُوا ۖ قَالُوا وَمَا لَنَا أَلَّا نُقَاتِلَ فِي سَبِيلِ اللَّهِ وَقَدْ أُخْرِجْنَا مِن دِيَارِنَا وَأَبْنَائِنَا ۖ فَلَمَّا كُتِبَ عَلَيْهِمُ الْقِتَالُ تَوَلَّوْا إِلَّا قَلِيلًا مِّنْهُمْ ۗ وَاللَّهُ عَلِيمٌ بِالظَّالِمِينَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse shifts to recount a story from the history of the Children of Israel after the time of Prophet Moses. It tells of a time when their elders asked a prophet among them to appoint a king to lead them in fighting for Allah's cause, claiming they had been driven from their homes and families. The prophet questioned their sincerity, asking if they might refuse to fight if it were actually commanded. Despite their strong declaration of readiness due to their suffering, when fighting was ordained, most of them turned away, except for a small group. The verse concludes by stating that Allah is fully aware of those who turn away from their commitments.

Verse 247

وَقَالَ لَهُمْ نَبِيُّهُمْ إِنَّ اللَّهَ قَدْ بَعَثَ لَكُمْ طَالُوتَ مَلِكًا ۚ قَالُوا أَنَّىٰ يَكُونُ لَهُ الْمُلْكُ عَلَيْنَا وَنَحْنُ أَحَقُّ بِالْمُلْكِ مِنْهُ وَلَمْ يُؤْتَ سَعَةً مِّنَ الْمَالِ ۚ قَالَ إِنَّ اللَّهَ اصْطَفَاهُ عَلَيْكُمْ وَزَادَهُ بَسْطَةً فِي الْعِلْمِ وَالْجِسْمِ ۖ وَاللَّهُ يُؤْتِي مُلْكَهُ مَن يَشَاءُ ۚ وَاللَّهُ وَاسِعٌ عَلِيمٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: After the Children of Israel asked for a king, their prophet told them that Allah had appointed Talut (Saul) to lead them. Instead of accepting this divine choice, they objected, claiming others were more deserving because Talut lacked wealth and high social status. Their prophet explained that Allah had chosen him for his knowledge and physical strength—key qualities for leadership, especially in times of battle. He reminded them that Allah grants authority to whomever He wills. This verse highlights that true leadership is based on divine wisdom, not worldly measures like lineage or wealth.

Verse 248

وَقَالَ لَهُمْ نَبِيُّهُمْ إِنَّ آيَةَ مُلْكِهِ أَن يَأْتِيَكُمُ التَّابُوتُ فِيهِ سَكِينَةٌ مِّن رَّبِّكُمْ وَبَقِيَّةٌ مِّمَّا تَرَكَ آلُ مُوسَىٰ وَآلُ هَارُونَ تَحْمِلُهُ الْمَلَائِكَةُ ۚ إِنَّ فِي ذَٰلِكَ لَآيَةً لَّكُمْ إِن كُنتُم مُّؤْمِنِينَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: As further evidence of Talut’s (Saul’s) appointment by Allah, their prophet gave them a physical sign: the return of the Ark of the Covenant. This Ark was a sacred object containing relics from the families of Prophet Moses and Prophet Aaron. In it there is peace and reassurance from their Lord. The verse describes the Ark being carried by angels. Their prophet told them this would be a clear sign of Talut’s authority, if they were truly believers. This act of divine intervention was meant to remove any doubt about Talut’s divinely appointed leadership.

Verse 249

فَلَمَّا فَصَلَ طَالُوتُ بِالْجُنُودِ قَالَ إِنَّ اللَّهَ مُبْتَلِيكُم بِنَهَرٍ فَمَن شَرِبَ مِنْهُ فَلَيْسَ مِنِّي وَمَن لَّمْ يَطْعَمْهُ فَإِنَّهُ مِنِّي إِلَّا مَنِ اغْتَرَفَ غُرْفَةً بِيَدِهِ ۚ فَشَرِبُوا مِنْهُ إِلَّا قَلِيلًا مِّنْهُمْ ۚ فَلَمَّا جَاوَزَهُ هُوَ وَالَّذِينَ آمَنُوا مَعَهُ قَالُوا لَا طَاقَةَ لَنَا الْيَوْمَ بِجَالُوتَ وَجُنُودِهِ ۚ قَالَ الَّذِينَ يَظُنُّونَ أَنَّهُم مُّلَاقُو اللَّهِ كَم مِّن فِئَةٍ قَلِيلَةٍ غَلَبَتْ فِئَةً كَثِيرَةً بِإِذْنِ اللَّهِ ۗ وَاللَّهُ مَعَ الصَّابِرِينَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: After being appointed king, Talut (Saul) led his army and informed them that Allah would test them with a river: they were not to drink from it except for a small sip. Despite this clear warning, almost all of them failed by drinking their fill, leaving only a small number who passed the test through discipline and obedience. Later, when this group crossed the river and faced the much larger army of their enemy, Goliath, many among them felt overwhelmed and doubted their chances of victory. However, a very small core—those who were certain they would meet Allah—reminded the others that victory comes by Allah’s will, and that Allah is with those who are patient and steadfast. This moment highlights that true strength lies not in numbers, but in faith, discipline, and trust in Allah.

Verse 250

وَلَمَّا بَرَزُوا لِجَالُوتَ وَجُنُودِهِ قَالُوا رَبَّنَا أَفْرِغْ عَلَيْنَا صَبْرًا وَثَبِّتْ أَقْدَامَنَا وَانصُرْنَا عَلَى الْقَوْمِ الْكَافِرِينَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: As the small, faithful group under Talut (Saul) advanced to face the much larger enemy force led by Goliath, they turned to Allah in sincere prayer. They asked Him to grant them patience and steadfastness in the face of adversity, to keep their feet firm during the battle, and to grant them victory over the disbelieving forces. This prayer reveals their deep reliance on Allah for strength and success, recognizing that true victory comes only from Him.

Verse 251

فَهَزَمُوهُم بِإِذْنِ اللَّهِ وَقَتَلَ دَاوُودُ جَالُوتَ وَآتَاهُ اللَّهُ الْمُلْكَ وَالْحِكْمَةَ وَعَلَّمَهُ مِمَّا يَشَاءُ ۗ وَلَوْلَا دَفْعُ اللَّهِ النَّاسَ بَعْضَهُم بِبَعْضٍ لَّفَسَدَتِ الْأَرْضُ وَلَـٰكِنَّ اللَّهَ ذُو فَضْلٍ عَلَى الْعَالَمِينَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: The verse narrates the outcome: by Allah’s will, the small group routed the enemy forces. It specifically mentions that David, who was part of Talut’s (Saul’s) army, killed Goliath. Following this victory, Allah granted David kingship and wisdom and taught him as He willed. This shows that victory and authority come from Allah alone, and that Allah chooses whom He blesses with leadership and knowledge. The verse then explains an important universal principle about how groups or nations interact. When any group becomes powerful and strong, it might be tempted to act unjustly or exceed limits. To prevent this, Allah allows another group to rise up and balance the first. This ongoing balance helps stop corruption and chaos from spreading and serves a vital purpose in preventing greater evil. Finally, the verse reminds us that Allah is full of great bounty and favor towards all creation.

Verse 252

تِلْكَ آيَاتُ اللَّهِ نَتْلُوهَا عَلَيْكَ بِالْحَقِّ ۚ وَإِنَّكَ لَمِنَ الْمُرْسَلِينَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This final verse in the passage affirms that the earlier stories and lessons are “Signs of Allah” — meaning they are divine messages filled with wisdom and guidance for humanity. It stresses that these accounts are true and were revealed to the Prophet Muhammad. By saying, “verily Thou art one of the messengers,” the verse confirms that Muhammad is indeed a messenger chosen by Allah to receive these revelations and to deliver Allah’s guidance to people. This establishes the Prophet’s role as a trustworthy conveyor of Allah’s divine signs.

Verse 253

تِلْكَ الرُّسُلُ فَضَّلْنَا بَعْضَهُمْ عَلَىٰ بَعْضٍ ۘ مِّنْهُم مَّن كَلَّمَ اللَّهُ ۖ وَرَفَعَ بَعْضَهُمْ دَرَجَاتٍ ۚ وَآتَيْنَا عِيسَى ابْنَ مَرْيَمَ الْبَيِّنَاتِ وَأَيَّدْنَاهُ بِرُوحِ الْقُدُسِ ۗ وَلَوْ شَاءَ اللَّهُ مَا اقْتَتَلَ الَّذِينَ مِن بَعْدِهِم مِّن بَعْدِ مَا جَاءَتْهُمُ الْبَيِّنَاتُ وَلَـٰكِنِ اخْتَلَفُوا فَمِنْهُم مَّنْ آمَنَ وَمِنْهُم مَّن كَفَرَ ۚ وَلَوْ شَاءَ اللَّهُ مَا اقْتَتَلُوا وَلَـٰكِنَّ اللَّهَ يَفْعَلُ مَا يُرِيدُ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse speaks about the messengers sent by Allah—special individuals chosen to deliver His message. All these messengers were united in conveying the core message of Allah’s oneness and guidance. The verse notes that Allah elevated some messengers above others in rank and status. For example, one messenger was spoken to directly by Allah (commonly understood as Prophet Moses), others were given higher ranks, and Prophet Jesus, the son of Mary, was given clear signs (such as miracles) and was strengthened by the Holy Spirit (understood as the Archangel Gabriel). Despite the clear messages and signs brought by these prophets, their followers and later generations differed in their beliefs—some accepted the faith, while others rejected it. This division sometimes led to conflicts and fighting. Muslims should learn from this and avoid similar divisions and disputes. Furthermore, the verse implies that if Allah had willed, there would be no conflict—just as angels do not differ or rebel. However, Allah created human beings on earth as a test, and the prophets were not sent as enforcers or policemen to force obedience. Instead, they came with clear signs and reasonable arguments inviting people to righteousness. If Allah had so willed, there would have been no fighting. Ultimately, all is within Allah’s control.

Verse 254

يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا أَنفِقُوا مِمَّا رَزَقْنَاكُم مِّن قَبْلِ أَن يَأْتِيَ يَوْمٌ لَّا بَيْعٌ فِيهِ وَلَا خُلَّةٌ وَلَا شَفَاعَةٌ ۗ وَالْكَافِرُونَ هُمُ الظَّالِمُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Following the discussion about the different messengers and the divisions among their followers, this verse now speaks directly to the believers. It urges them to give—spend from the wealth that Allah has provided them—before the coming of a specific Day: the Day of Judgment. On that Day, no bargaining, friendship, or intercession will help. Every individual will be entirely focused on their own soul, and emotional or social ties from this world will no longer hold weight. The verse warns that worldly means of influence or support will be meaningless in the Hereafter. Those who reject faith are described as wrongdoers—people who either harm themselves by turning away from truth or harm others by leading them away from Allah’s guidance. This verse builds a bridge from the earlier discussion of prophets and their communities to a direct ethical call for the present-day believers: to act responsibly, give generously, and prepare for the Day when only sincere faith and deeds will matter.

Verse 255

اللَّهُ لَا إِلَـٰهَ إِلَّا هُوَ الْحَيُّ الْقَيُّومُ ۚ لَا تَأْخُذُهُ سِنَةٌ وَلَا نَوْمٌ ۚ لَّهُ مَا فِي السَّمَاوَاتِ وَمَا فِي الْأَرْضِ ۗ مَن ذَا الَّذِي يَشْفَعُ عِندَهُ إِلَّا بِإِذْنِهِ ۚ يَعْلَمُ مَا بَيْنَ أَيْدِيهِمْ وَمَا خَلْفَهُمْ ۖ وَلَا يُحِيطُونَ بِشَيْءٍ مِّنْ عِلْمِهِ إِلَّا بِمَا شَاءَ ۚ وَسِعَ كُرْسِيُّهُ السَّمَاوَاتِ وَالْأَرْضَ ۖ وَلَا يَئُودُهُ حِفْظُهُمَا ۚ وَهُوَ الْعَلِيُّ الْعَظِيمُ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This powerful verse, known as Ayat al-Kursi (the Verse of the Throne), summarizes key Islamic beliefs about Allah’s nature and attributes. It begins by affirming that only Allah is worthy of worship. He is described as the Ever-Living and the Sustainer of all existence—unaffected by drowsiness or sleep. Everything in the heavens and the earth belongs to Him, highlighting His absolute ownership and control. The verse emphasizes that no one can intercede with Allah without His permission. It affirms His perfect knowledge of all things—past, present, and future—while human knowledge is limited to what He allows. It also mentions that Allah’s Kursi (commonly translated as "Seat" or "Throne") extends over the heavens and the earth. Early scholars affirmed that the Kursi is a real entity, but its exact nature is beyond human understanding. Since Allah is absolutely unique—as stated in verse 42:11, “There is nothing like Him”—the Kursi must also be understood in a way that reflects this uniqueness. Just as Allah has attributes like knowledge, hearing, and seeing that are unlike those of creation, so too is His Kursi beyond comparison. The verse concludes by stating that sustaining the heavens and the earth does not tire or burden Him. Truly, He is the Most High and the Most Great. This verse presents a profound summary of Allah’s majesty, authority, and transcendence.

Verse 256

لَا إِكْرَاهَ فِي الدِّينِ ۖ قَد تَّبَيَّنَ الرُّشْدُ مِنَ الْغَيِّ ۚ فَمَن يَكْفُرْ بِالطَّاغُوتِ وَيُؤْمِن بِاللَّهِ فَقَدِ اسْتَمْسَكَ بِالْعُرْوَةِ الْوُثْقَىٰ لَا انفِصَامَ لَهَا ۗ وَاللَّهُ سَمِيعٌ عَلِيمٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Building upon the absolute and unique nature of Allah described in the previous verse, this verse establishes a fundamental principle: there is to be no compulsion in matters of religion. This is because the truth of belief in Allah is made distinct and clear from error. Accepting faith is a matter of personal conviction, addressing the human mind, emotions, and inherent nature. True faith involves rejecting "taghut" (a term referring to anything that exceeds its bounds and seeks to dominate, including tyranny or false deities) and believing solely in Allah. The verse uses the image of grasping a "most trustworthy hand-hold" that never breaks to describe the security and certainty gained by someone who rejects falsehood and believes in Allah. It concludes by reaffirming that Allah hears everything and knows all things, both outwardly expressed and hidden within people's hearts.

Verse 257

اللَّهُ وَلِيُّ الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا يُخْرِجُهُم مِّنَ الظُّلُمَاتِ إِلَى النُّورِ ۖ وَالَّذِينَ كَفَرُوا أَوْلِيَاؤُهُمُ الطَّاغُوتُ يُخْرِجُونَهُم مِّنَ النُّورِ إِلَى الظُّلُمَاتِ ۗ أُولَـٰئِكَ أَصْحَابُ النَّارِ ۖ هُمْ فِيهَا خَالِدُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Following the declaration of “no compulsion in religion” and the clear distinction between truth and error, this verse vividly illustrates the contrasting outcomes of belief and disbelief. It explains that Allah is the protector and guide of those who believe. He brings them out of various forms of darkness—such as ignorance, confusion, and misguidance—into the singular light of faith in Him and adherence to His guidance. In contrast, those who reject faith are supported by false powers (ṭāghūt), a term referring to anything that oversteps its bounds—such as tyrants, false deities, or misleading ideologies. These influences draw them away from the light of innate understanding and divine clarity into multiple layers of darkness. These darknesses may include selfish desires, spiritual deviation, oppression, or blind submission, ultimately leading to loss in the afterlife. The verse powerfully uses the singular word “light” (nūr) to represent the unified and consistent truth of faith, and the plural “darknesses” (ẓulumāt) to express the many divergent paths of falsehood and misguidance.

Verse 258

أَلَمْ تَرَ إِلَى الَّذِي حَاجَّ إِبْرَاهِيمَ فِي رَبِّهِ أَنْ آتَاهُ اللَّهُ الْمُلْكَ إِذْ قَالَ إِبْرَاهِيمُ رَبِّيَ الَّذِي يُحْيِي وَيُمِيتُ قَالَ أَنَا أُحْيِي وَأُمِيتُ ۖ قَالَ إِبْرَاهِيمُ فَإِنَّ اللَّهَ يَأْتِي بِالشَّمْسِ مِنَ الْمَشْرِقِ فَأْتِ بِهَا مِنَ الْمَغْرِبِ فَبُهِتَ الَّذِي كَفَرَ ۗ وَاللَّهُ لَا يَهْدِي الْقَوْمَ الظَّالِمِينَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse recounts a debate between Prophet Abraham and a king who arrogantly argued with him about the existence and authority of Allah. Though the king had been granted worldly power by Allah, he used it to challenge divine authority. When Abraham said, “My Lord is the one who gives life and causes death,” the king claimed he could do the same—likely referring to his ability to pardon or execute people. To refute this superficial claim, Abraham posed a deeper challenge: “Indeed, Allah causes the sun to rise from the east—so bring it up from the west.” This exposed the king’s limitations. His authority extended only over people, not over the laws of nature or creation. Confronted with this irrefutable logic, the disbeliever was silenced. The verse concludes by affirming that Allah does not guide the unjust, particularly those who reject truth out of pride or stubbornness. This story underscores a central message: true power and sovereignty belong only to Allah, and those in positions of power must acknowledge and submit to Him. It continues the theme of divine authority that runs through the preceding verses.

Verse 259

أَوْ كَالَّذِي مَرَّ عَلَىٰ قَرْيَةٍ وَهِيَ خَاوِيَةٌ عَلَىٰ عُرُوشِهَا قَالَ أَنَّىٰ يُحْيِي هَـٰذِهِ اللَّهُ بَعْدَ مَوْتِهَا ۖ فَأَمَاتَهُ اللَّهُ مِائَةَ عَامٍ ثُمَّ بَعَثَهُ ۖ قَالَ كَمْ لَبِثْتَ ۖ قَالَ لَبِثْتُ يَوْمًا أَوْ بَعْضَ يَوْمٍ ۖ قَالَ بَل لَّبِثْتَ مِائَةَ عَامٍ فَانظُرْ إِلَىٰ طَعَامِكَ وَشَرَابِكَ لَمْ يَتَسَنَّهْ ۖ وَانظُرْ إِلَىٰ حِمَارِكَ وَلِنَجْعَلَكَ آيَةً لِّلنَّاسِ ۖ وَانظُرْ إِلَى الْعِظَامِ كَيْفَ نُنشِزُهَا ثُمَّ نَكْسُوهَا لَحْمًا ۚ فَلَمَّا تَبَيَّنَ لَهُ قَالَ أَعْلَمُ أَنَّ اللَّهَ عَلَىٰ كُلِّ شَيْءٍ قَدِيرٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Continuing the theme of Allah’s power over life and death, this verse presents another vivid example. It tells of a man who passed by a desolate, ruined town and wondered how Allah could ever bring it back to life after such devastation. In response, Allah caused the man to die for a hundred years and then revived him, as a demonstration of divine power. Upon being resurrected, the man was asked how long he thought he had remained in that state. He answered that it felt like a day or part of a day. Allah informed him that he had, in fact, been dead for a hundred years and provided signs to make this clear: his food and drink remained untouched by time, while his donkey had decayed to bones. This miraculous contrast—where time was suspended for some things and accelerated for others—showed that Allah alone governs time. He can preserve or decay as He wills, unaffected by natural laws. Time itself is a creation subject to His command. Then, Allah visibly reassembled the donkey’s bones, clothed them with flesh, and brought it back to life before the man’s eyes. Witnessing this, the man exclaimed, “Now I know that Allah has power over all things.” This narrative reinforces the concept of Allah's absolute power over creation, including bringing the dead back to life, following the discussion in the previous verse.

Verse 260

وَإِذْ قَالَ إِبْرَاهِيمُ رَبِّ أَرِنِي كَيْفَ تُحْيِي الْمَوْتَىٰ ۖ قَالَ أَوَلَمْ تُؤْمِن ۖ قَالَ بَلَىٰ وَلَـٰكِن لِّيَطْمَئِنَّ قَلْبِي ۖ قَالَ فَخُذْ أَرْبَعَةً مِّنَ الطَّيْرِ فَصُرْهُنَّ إِلَيْكَ ثُمَّ اجْعَلْ عَلَىٰ كُلِّ جَبَلٍ مِّنْهُنَّ جُزْءًا ثُمَّ ادْعُهُنَّ يَأْتِينَكَ سَعْيًا ۚ وَاعْلَمْ أَنَّ اللَّهَ عَزِيزٌ حَكِيمٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse returns to Prophet Abraham, who asks Allah to show him how He brings the dead back to life. Allah asks him if he lacks faith, to which Prophet Abraham responds that his faith is strong, but he wishes for this experience to bring greater reassurance and inner peace—something attained through direct, personal observation. In other words, he sought to witness the process of resurrection with his own eyes. In response, Allah instructs Prophet Abraham to take four birds, make sure he is familiar with them, then cut them into pieces and scatter parts of their bodies on different mountain tops. He was then instructed to call the birds back to him. The verse states that they came swiftly to him, fully alive again. This was a direct, visible demonstration of Allah's power to resurrect the dead, showing that He can reassemble and give life to fragmented remains across distances. The verse concludes by reminding us to know that Allah is "Almighty" (possessing ultimate power) and "Wise" (acting with perfect knowledge and purpose). Like the previous verse, this story serves as another powerful illustration of Allah's absolute power over life and death, confirming that resurrection is entirely within His capability, as He is the source of all creation and life.

Verse 261

مَّثَلُ الَّذِينَ يُنفِقُونَ أَمْوَالَهُمْ فِي سَبِيلِ اللَّهِ كَمَثَلِ حَبَّةٍ أَنبَتَتْ سَبْعَ سَنَابِلَ فِي كُلِّ سُنبُلَةٍ مِّائَةُ حَبَّةٍ ۗ وَاللَّهُ يُضَاعِفُ لِمَن يَشَاءُ ۗ وَاللَّهُ وَاسِعٌ عَلِيمٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse begins by offering a beautiful comparison for those who spend their wealth for the sake of Allah. It is like a single grain of corn that grows into seven ears, with each ear bearing a hundred grains. This vivid image illustrates the abundant, multiplying growth and life that results from sincere charity. It emphasizes that Allah can increase this reward many times over for whomever He wills. We understand from this that Allah's bounty is vast and unlimited. The verse concludes by affirming that Allah is All-Bountiful and knows all things, including people's intentions when they give.

Verse 262

الَّذِينَ يُنفِقُونَ أَمْوَالَهُمْ فِي سَبِيلِ اللَّهِ ثُمَّ لَا يُتْبِعُونَ مَا أَنفَقُوا مَنًّا وَلَا أَذًى ۙ لَّهُمْ أَجْرُهُمْ عِندَ رَبِّهِمْ وَلَا خَوْفٌ عَلَيْهِمْ وَلَا هُمْ يَحْزَنُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Following the previous verse's powerful image of multiplied reward, this verse explains who receives this generous increase. It specifies that the reward is for those who spend for Allah's cause and, crucially, do not spoil their act of giving by reminding the recipient of their generosity or by causing them harm. This highlights a key principle of faith: the giver should forget their act of giving and avoid displaying it, even to close family members—especially children who may not fully understand the spiritual purpose behind charity. For example, a child who sees their parent giving something to a neighbor might later tease the neighbor’s child or boast about the family’s generosity in a way that humiliates others. The reward for such sincere and discreet giving is assured with Allah. Furthermore, those who give in this respectful manner are promised freedom from fear and grief. This connects to the previous verse by clarifying the conditions under which the potential for manifold increase is fully realized.

Verse 263

قَوْلٌ مَّعْرُوفٌ وَمَغْفِرَةٌ خَيْرٌ مِّن صَدَقَةٍ يَتْبَعُهَا أَذًى ۗ وَاللَّهُ غَنِيٌّ حَلِيمٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Building upon the theme of the previous verse, which warned against harming the recipient of charity, this verse emphasizes the importance of good manners and compassion. It states that speaking kind words and forgiving others is better in Allah's sight than giving charity that is followed by hurtful behavior. Kind speech here includes turning down a beggar in a gentle manner, without making them feel ashamed or blamed for asking. If the beggar responds harshly, as people in deep need sometimes do, one should be patient and forgiving. Why? Because a needy person may see that you are well-off and hope for help, and in desperation, they may speak bluntly or even rudely. In such cases, believers are urged to respond with tolerance and mercy. This verse highlights that the manner in which charity is given—and one’s overall conduct—matters greatly. It concludes by reminding us that Allah is free of all needs and is Most Forbearing, setting a divine example of patience and generosity for believers to follow.

Verse 264

يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا لَا تُبْطِلُوا صَدَقَاتِكُم بِالْمَنِّ وَالْأَذَىٰ كَالَّذِي يُنفِقُ مَالَهُ رِئَاءَ النَّاسِ وَلَا يُؤْمِنُ بِاللَّهِ وَالْيَوْمِ الْآخِرِ ۖ فَمَثَلُهُ كَمَثَلِ صَفْوَانٍ عَلَيْهِ تُرَابٌ فَأَصَابَهُ وَابِلٌ فَتَرَكَهُ صَلْدًا ۖ لَّا يَقْدِرُونَ عَلَىٰ شَيْءٍ مِّمَّا كَسَبُوا ۗ وَاللَّهُ لَا يَهْدِي الْقَوْمَ الْكَافِرِينَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse directly addresses the believers, warning them not to nullify the reward of their charity by boasting about it or causing harm—echoing the theme of the previous verses (262 and 263). It offers a stark comparison: those who spend their wealth not for Allah’s sake, but merely to be seen and praised by others, while lacking true faith in Allah and the Last Day. Such people are likened to a hard, smooth rock covered with a thin layer of soil. When heavy rain falls, the soil is washed away, leaving the bare rock behind—barren and unable to support growth. Similarly, their actions, though they may appear generous outwardly, are devoid of sincere faith and thus leave no lasting spiritual reward. Allah makes their deeds as worthless as dust blown by the wind. This powerful image illustrates that good deeds without sincere intention and faith are hollow. Allah does not guide those who deny belief, nor does He grant reward to actions done for show.

Verse 265

وَمَثَلُ الَّذِينَ يُنفِقُونَ أَمْوَالَهُمُ ابْتِغَاءَ مَرْضَاتِ اللَّهِ وَتَثْبِيتًا مِّنْ أَنفُسِهِمْ كَمَثَلِ جَنَّةٍ بِرَبْوَةٍ أَصَابَهَا وَابِلٌ فَآتَتْ أُكُلَهَا ضِعْفَيْنِ فَإِن لَّمْ يُصِبْهَا وَابِلٌ فَطَلٌّ ۗ وَاللَّهُ بِمَا تَعْمَلُونَ بَصِيرٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: In contrast to the barren rock described in the previous verse, this verse offers a powerful image of the sincere giver. Those who spend their wealth seeking only Allah’s approval and as an affirmation of their own faith are likened to a fertile garden situated on a hill. This garden yields abundantly, doubling its produce with heavy rain, and even a light drizzle—i.e., soft rain—is sufficient for its growth. This illustrates how sincere charity, rooted in faith, is always fruitful and blessed, regardless of the amount given. It produces great results in terms of reward and spiritual growth. The verse concludes by affirming that Allah sees and is fully aware of everything people do, including the sincerity behind their giving. This reinforces the connection to the previous verse by presenting the positive outcome of giving with the right intention, directly contrasting the negative outcome of insincere giving.

Verse 266

أَيَوَدُّ أَحَدُكُمْ أَن تَكُونَ لَهُ جَنَّةٌ مِّن نَّخِيلٍ وَأَعْنَابٍ تَجْرِي مِن تَحْتِهَا الْأَنْهَارُ لَهُ فِيهَا مِن كُلِّ الثَّمَرَاتِ وَأَصَابَهُ الْكِبَرُ وَلَهُ ذُرِّيَّةٌ ضُعَفَاءُ فَأَصَابَهَا إِعْصَارٌ فِيهِ نَارٌ فَاحْتَرَقَتْ ۗ كَذَٰلِكَ يُبَيِّنُ اللَّهُ لَكُمُ الْآيَاتِ لَعَلَّكُمْ تَتَفَكَّرُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse uses another moving parable to emphasize the value and vulnerability of one’s deeds. It asks whether anyone would wish to own a flourishing garden—with palm trees, vines, and flowing streams, producing abundant fruits—only to see it completely destroyed by a fiery whirlwind at the very moment they become old, weak, and unable to support their dependent children. The lush garden symbolizes a lifetime of good deeds—charity, prayer, devotion—accumulated with apparent care and consistency. The old age and dependent children represent a person in a state of utmost need and vulnerability, symbolizing the time of death or the Day of Judgment, when one most depends on those good deeds to secure salvation. The fiery whirlwind destroying the garden reflects the nullification of those deeds due to harmful intentions such as insincerity, showing off, causing hurt, or following charity with reminders and arrogance, as highlighted in verses 262–265. This parable illustrates how even a lifetime of effort can be undone if not grounded in sincerity and humility. Allah presents such parables clearly so that people may reflect deeply on the consequences of their actions. It reinforces the preceding discussion by warning that good works must be built upon a solid foundation of sincere faith and upright conduct in order to truly endure.

Verse 267

يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا أَنفِقُوا مِن طَيِّبَاتِ مَا كَسَبْتُمْ وَمِمَّا أَخْرَجْنَا لَكُم مِّنَ الْأَرْضِ ۖ وَلَا تَيَمَّمُوا الْخَبِيثَ مِنْهُ تُنفِقُونَ وَلَسْتُم بِآخِذِيهِ إِلَّا أَن تُغْمِضُوا فِيهِ ۚ وَاعْلَمُوا أَنَّ اللَّهَ غَنِيٌّ حَمِيدٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Shifting from the manner and intention of giving, this verse addresses the quality of what is given. It directs believers to give in charity from the good and lawful wealth they have earned or from the good produce that Allah has provided from the earth. Allah emphasizes the true source of provision by saying, "and from that which We have brought forth for you from the earth." Upon reflecting on the phrase "Spend from the good things which you have earned," we see that Allah honors and acknowledges human effort. However, earning is not the ultimate source of provision. Rather, the ability to earn is itself a gift from Allah: you can only work because of the energy He grants you, the intellect He bestows, and the earth He has made accessible. The many tools and faculties involved in earning are all blessings from Him. The verse strongly prohibits the practice of giving inferior or unwanted items in charity—things one would not accept for oneself except with reluctance or disdain. This sets a high ethical standard for giving, grounded in respect for both Allah and the recipient. The verse concludes by affirming that Allah is free of all needs and worthy of all praise—reminding us that He does not need our charity, but that its benefit returns to us. This builds on the earlier concept of giving for Allah’s sake by adding the essential dimension of offering the best of what one possesses.

Verse 268

الشَّيْطَانُ يَعِدُكُمُ الْفَقْرَ وَيَأْمُرُكُم بِالْفَحْشَاءِ ۖ وَاللَّهُ يَعِدُكُم مَّغْفِرَةً مِّنْهُ وَفَضْلًا ۗ وَاللَّهُ وَاسِعٌ عَلِيمٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse speaks directly to the psychological barriers that can prevent generous giving. It reveals that Satan tries to frighten people with the fear of poverty if they spend in charity. He also encourages them toward indecent or shameful behavior—such as stinginess, hoarding, or pursuing wealth unjustly. In clear contrast, Allah promises forgiveness for shortcomings and abundant bounty to those who follow His path. This highlights a central tension: whether one succumbs to Satan’s fear-mongering or places their trust in Allah’s promise of provision and reward. The verse encourages believers to overcome hesitation and give freely. The verse concludes by affirming that Allah is All-Bountiful and All-Knowing, reassuring believers that He is fully aware of their struggles against temptation and their sincere efforts to do good. This directly connects to the previous verse’s directive to give from the best of what one has, by addressing the inner fear that may hinder generosity.

Verse 269

يُؤْتِي الْحِكْمَةَ مَن يَشَاءُ ۚ وَمَن يُؤْتَ الْحِكْمَةَ فَقَدْ أُوتِيَ خَيْرًا كَثِيرًا ۗ وَمَا يَذَّكَّرُ إِلَّا أُولُو الْأَلْبَابِ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse addresses the gift of wisdom, which can be understood as the ability to perceive, internalize, and apply the deeper truths and guidance revealed by Allah—including principles related to giving and the purpose of life. It states that Allah grants this profound gift to whomever He wills. Whoever receives wisdom has indeed been granted an immense and overflowing benefit. The verse emphasizes that only those with genuine understanding and sound judgment are truly able to grasp the significance of Allah’s guidance—such as the contrast between the path of Allah and the misleading path promoted by Satan mentioned in the previous verse.

Verse 270

وَمَا أَنفَقْتُم مِّن نَّفَقَةٍ أَوْ نَذَرْتُم مِّن نَّذْرٍ فَإِنَّ اللَّهَ يَعْلَمُهُ ۗ وَمَا لِلظَّالِمِينَ مِنْ أَنصَارٍ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse provides both reassurance and warning. It affirms that whatever believers spend—whether through obligatory charity (like Zakat), voluntary donations, or other devotional acts—Allah is fully aware of it. No sincere effort goes unnoticed by Him. The verse also mentions fulfilling vows. A vow refers to voluntarily committing to an extra act of worship that is not obligatory but still legislated by Allah—such as praying more than the five required daily prayers. While making a vow is optional, once made, it becomes a binding duty. This highlights the seriousness of such commitments and the wisdom in not making vows lightly, as one may later struggle to fulfill them. Finally, the verse contrasts the condition of believers with that of wrongdoers. While believers are assured that Allah sees and rewards their sincere deeds, wrongdoers—those who ignore or violate their duties—will have no helpers. This serves as a powerful reminder of personal accountability and Allah’s complete knowledge of our actions.

Verse 271

إِن تُبْدُوا الصَّدَقَاتِ فَنِعِمَّا هِيَ ۖ وَإِن تُخْفُوهَا وَتُؤْتُوهَا الْفُقَرَاءَ فَهُوَ خَيْرٌ لَّكُمْ ۚ وَيُكَفِّرُ عَنكُم مِّن سَيِّئَاتِكُمْ ۗ وَاللَّهُ بِمَا تَعْمَلُونَ خَبِيرٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse continues the discussion on giving charity, building on the previous point about the importance of intention. It explains that while giving charity openly is acceptable, doing so discreetly, particularly for voluntary acts, is often better. Giving charity privately helps ensure sincerity and avoids any potential for showing off or embarrassing the recipient. However, giving charity openly is also good and can encourage others to do the same. Regardless of whether charity is given openly or in secret, Allah is fully aware of the act and the intention behind it. The verse also mentions that giving charity can help purify a person and reduce the burden of their shortcomings.

Verse 272

لَّيْسَ عَلَيْكَ هُدَاهُمْ وَلَـٰكِنَّ اللَّهَ يَهْدِي مَن يَشَاءُ ۗ وَمَا تُنفِقُوا مِنْ خَيْرٍ فَلِأَنفُسِكُمْ ۚ وَمَا تُنفِقُونَ إِلَّا ابْتِغَاءَ وَجْهِ اللَّهِ ۚ وَمَا تُنفِقُوا مِنْ خَيْرٍ يُوَفَّ إِلَيْكُمْ وَأَنتُمْ لَا تُظْلَمُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse addresses the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ, clarifying that his responsibility is to convey the message of Allah, but the guidance of hearts is entirely in Allah’s control. It emphasizes that true faith and guidance are blessings that come only from Allah. The verse then shifts back to the theme of charity, stating that acts of giving can be directed to anyone in need, regardless of their faith, as long as the intention is pure—seeking only Allah’s pleasure. It teaches that whatever good you do, including giving in charity, is ultimately for your own benefit. Spiritually, it elevates your soul and brings reward in the Hereafter. The verse reassures believers that no act of good will go unnoticed or unrewarded, and Allah will never wrong anyone in what they are due.

Verse 273

لِلْفُقَرَاءِ الَّذِينَ أُحْصِرُوا فِي سَبِيلِ اللَّهِ لَا يَسْتَطِيعُونَ ضَرْبًا فِي الْأَرْضِ يَحْسَبُهُمُ الْجَاهِلُ أَغْنِيَاءَ مِنَ التَّعَفُّفِ تَعْرِفُهُم بِسِيمَاهُمْ لَا يَسْأَلُونَ النَّاسَ إِلْحَافًا ۗ وَمَا تُنفِقُوا مِنْ خَيْرٍ فَإِنَّ اللَّهَ بِهِ عَلِيمٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse describes a specific group of people who are particularly deserving of charity. These are individuals who are in need but are unable to travel or work to earn a living—especially those whose hardship results from their commitment to serving Allah’s cause or the community. The verse highlights their dignity and self-respect: they remain modest and do not beg. Because of their restraint, people unaware of their reality may assume they are self-sufficient. However, the verse suggests that a perceptive person can recognize their situation. A believer is encouraged to develop insight and compassion toward fellow believers—to be able to recognize signs of need without requiring the person to ask. True perceptiveness in faith involves reading the expressions and conditions of others and responding before they are forced to beg. Failing to notice such signs reflects a lack of awareness and sensitivity. This verse concludes by assuring that any good given to these individuals is fully known to Allah, and He will certainly reward it. This continues the theme of sincere and thoughtful charity by identifying those most in need—yet most likely to be overlooked.

Verse 274

الَّذِينَ يُنفِقُونَ أَمْوَالَهُم بِاللَّيْلِ وَالنَّهَارِ سِرًّا وَعَلَانِيَةً فَلَهُمْ أَجْرُهُمْ عِندَ رَبِّهِمْ وَلَا خَوْفٌ عَلَيْهِمْ وَلَا هُمْ يَحْزَنُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse concludes the section on the ethics and rewards of charity with a powerful summary. It promises a great reward from Allah for those who give charity from their wealth—regardless of whether they do so by day or night, in secret or openly. This inclusiveness emphasizes that all sincere forms of giving are valued. The verse reassures believers that those who give in this manner will have their reward secured with Allah and will face no fear or grief in the Hereafter. It serves as strong encouragement, offering peace and security as the outcome of sincere charitable acts. This ties back to earlier verses that stressed pure intention, wise giving, and trust in Allah’s promise of reward.

Verse 275

الَّذِينَ يَأْكُلُونَ الرِّبَا لَا يَقُومُونَ إِلَّا كَمَا يَقُومُ الَّذِي يَتَخَبَّطُهُ الشَّيْطَانُ مِنَ الْمَسِّ ۚ ذَٰلِكَ بِأَنَّهُمْ قَالُوا إِنَّمَا الْبَيْعُ مِثْلُ الرِّبَا ۗ وَأَحَلَّ اللَّهُ الْبَيْعَ وَحَرَّمَ الرِّبَا ۚ فَمَن جَاءَهُ مَوْعِظَةٌ مِّن رَّبِّهِ فَانتَهَىٰ فَلَهُ مَا سَلَفَ وَأَمْرُهُ إِلَى اللَّهِ ۖ وَمَنْ عَادَ فَأُولَـٰئِكَ أَصْحَابُ النَّارِ ۖ هُمْ فِيهَا خَالِدُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse follows a detailed discussion on charity by introducing a stark contrast: the condemnation of usury (riba). While charity is rooted in generosity and selflessness, usury represents exploitation—charging interest on money lent, regardless of the borrower’s hardship. The connection between the two topics is intentional. Charity involves giving without seeking personal gain, whereas usury seeks profit without effort or risk. Morally, they stand at opposite ends: one uplifts the vulnerable, the other burdens them further. The verse uses a striking image to describe those who engage in usury: as if they are driven to madness by Satan’s touch. This vividly illustrates the spiritual and moral corruption caused by usury (riba). Some people attempt to justify usury by saying, “Trade is just like usury,” but the verse makes a clear distinction: Allah has permitted trade and forbidden usury (riba). Trade involves mutual risk and genuine effort, while usury (riba) guarantees profit to the lender regardless of the borrower’s outcome. For those who practiced usury before receiving this guidance, the verse offers a way out: they may retain what they earned previously, and their case rests with Allah. However, those who continue after receiving the warning are promised the Fire. The verse draws a firm moral and legal boundary between ethical commerce and exploitative lending, warning of serious consequences for those who cross it.

Verse 276

يَمْحَقُ اللَّهُ الرِّبَا وَيُرْبِي الصَّدَقَاتِ ۗ وَاللَّهُ لَا يُحِبُّ كُلَّ كَفَّارٍ أَثِيمٍ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Continuing the message from the previous verse, which contrasted usury with trade, this verse highlights a deeper moral contrast—between usury and charity, such as zakat and voluntary giving. It states that Allah removes blessing from usury, while He blesses and multiplies the reward of charitable acts. Outwardly, giving in charity seems to reduce one’s wealth. Yet, in reality, it brings unseen benefits—spiritually and even materially—both for the giver and for society. Usury, on the other hand, may appear profitable, but it ultimately undermines well-being and brings no lasting prosperity. The verse concludes by stating that Allah does not love those who are ungrateful and persist in wrongdoing. Usury can be understood as embodying both traits: ingratitude for Allah’s blessings and injustice toward others. Often, only those with surplus wealth—beyond their basic needs—are in a position to lend at interest. According to the Qur'an, such surplus is a gift from Allah. True gratitude for this bounty involves sharing it with others, just as Allah has been generous to us. But when someone uses their surplus to profit from the hardship of others, they show not thankfulness, but greed—and commit moral wrongdoing. In this light, usury is not simply a financial act; it is a spiritual and ethical failure. This verse reinforces the Qur’anic ethical framework, where generosity, fairness, and compassion are favored over exploitation. It reminds us that our financial choices shape not only society but also our relationship with Allah.

Verse 277

إِنَّ الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا وَعَمِلُوا الصَّالِحَاتِ وَأَقَامُوا الصَّلَاةَ وَآتَوُا الزَّكَاةَ لَهُمْ أَجْرُهُمْ عِندَ رَبِّهِمْ وَلَا خَوْفٌ عَلَيْهِمْ وَلَا هُمْ يَحْزَنُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Following the strong condemnation of usury and its practitioners in the preceding verses, this verse shifts focus to a contrasting group: those who live according to the core principles of Islam. It describes believers who have faith, perform righteous deeds, establish regular prayer, and give regular charity (zakat). These acts are presented as foundational to the Islamic way of life and stand in clear opposition to a system based on usury and exploitation. The verse promises that such individuals will receive their reward from Allah and that they will have “no fear, nor shall they grieve.” This expression reflects a deep inner peace and sense of security—both in this life and the next—as a direct result of their commitment to faith, worship, and social responsibility. Charity, in particular, fosters a supportive and compassionate society, making exploitative practices like usury unnecessary. The tranquility described here stands in stark contrast to the moral and emotional instability associated with usury. Instead of anxiety and injustice, a life built on submission to Allah’s guidance and mutual care leads to true contentment and lasting peace.

Verse 278

يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا اتَّقُوا اللَّهَ وَذَرُوا مَا بَقِيَ مِنَ الرِّبَا إِن كُنتُم مُّؤْمِنِينَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse speaks directly to the believers and reinforces the prohibition of usury. It stresses the importance of fearing Allah and being mindful of Him as the guiding motivation for action. The verse then issues a clear and firm command to abandon usury. The phrase “if you are indeed believers” serves as a test of true faith, indicating that giving up usury is a fundamental requirement. This command marked a pivotal moment for the early Muslim community, calling for an immediate shift to a system free from usury. It demonstrates that Islamic faith is meant to guide all aspects of life, including economic transactions.

Verse 279

فَإِن لَّمْ تَفْعَلُوا فَأْذَنُوا بِحَرْبٍ مِّنَ اللَّهِ وَرَسُولِهِ ۖ وَإِن تُبْتُمْ فَلَكُمْ رُءُوسُ أَمْوَالِكُمْ لَا تَظْلِمُونَ وَلَا تُظْلَمُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse follows the command to abandon usury and outlines the severe consequence of failing to comply. It delivers a powerful warning: those who persist in demanding usurious gains are, in effect, declaring war against Allah and His Messenger. This stark language underscores the seriousness of continuing in a practice that has been clearly forbidden. Yet, the verse immediately offers a path of mercy. If one repents and ceases dealing in usury, they are permitted to recover only their original capital—no more, no less. The guiding principle is stated clearly: “Deal not unjustly, and you shall not be dealt with unjustly.” This ensures fairness for both parties—the lender does not suffer a loss, and the borrower is not burdened by unjust increase. The aim is to promote a just and ethical financial system, rooted in Allah’s guidance, where exploitation is removed and fairness upheld. In contrast, a society built on usury suffers deep moral, emotional, and economic harm—leading to conflict, inequality, and instability.

Verse 280

وَإِن كَانَ ذُو عُسْرَةٍ فَنَظِرَةٌ إِلَىٰ مَيْسَرَةٍ ۚ وَأَن تَصَدَّقُوا خَيْرٌ لَّكُمْ ۖ إِن كُنتُمْ تَعْلَمُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Following the strong directives against usury and the reminder that only the principal may be claimed, this verse addresses situations where a debtor is genuinely unable to repay a loan due to financial hardship. It offers a merciful guideline, instructing creditors to grant the debtor more time until their situation improves and repayment becomes easier. Beyond simply granting a delay, the verse encourages an even better course of action: forgiving the debt altogether as an act of charity. It states that this is "best for you if you only knew," suggesting hidden benefits and rewards for the creditor—perhaps both in this life and certainly in the hereafter. This approach fosters an atmosphere of tolerance and compassion, standing in stark contrast to the harshness of usury. It reinforces the Islamic principle of mutual support and highlights the community’s duty to assist those in genuine difficulty, discouraging any form of harassment or pressure against an insolvent debtor.

Verse 281

وَاتَّقُوا يَوْمًا تُرْجَعُونَ فِيهِ إِلَى اللَّهِ ۖ ثُمَّ تُوَفَّىٰ كُلُّ نَفْسٍ مَّا كَسَبَتْ وَهُمْ لَا يُظْلَمُونَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse serves as a powerful concluding reminder, shifting focus from worldly transactions and their consequences to the ultimate accountability in the hereafter. It urges everyone to "fear the Day when ye shall be brought back to Allah." This refers to the Day of Judgment, when all individuals will stand before Allah to be judged for their actions in this life. The verse emphasizes a fundamental principle of that Day: "every soul shall be paid what it earned," meaning each person will receive the full consequence of their deeds, whether good or bad. Crucially, it adds, "and none shall be dealt with unjustly." This assurance highlights Allah's perfect justice, where no one will be wronged by receiving less reward than they deserve or more punishment than they earned. The reminder about the fearful Day of Judgment can serve as a strong motivation, particularly in the context of financial dealings discussed previously, encouraging people to act justly and charitably, knowing they will be held accountable for their choices regarding wealth and their treatment of others. It underscores that living according to Allah's guidance, is ultimately for one's own benefit on the Day of Reckoning.

Verse 282

يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا إِذَا تَدَايَنتُم بِدَيْنٍ إِلَىٰ أَجَلٍ مُّسَمًّى فَاكْتُبُوهُ ۚ وَلْيَكْتُب بَّيْنَكُمْ كَاتِبٌ بِالْعَدْلِ ۚ وَلَا يَأْبَ كَاتِبٌ أَن يَكْتُبَ كَمَا عَلَّمَهُ اللَّهُ ۚ فَلْيَكْتُبْ وَلْيُمْلِلِ الَّذِي عَلَيْهِ الْحَقُّ وَلْيَتَّقِ اللَّهَ رَبَّهُ وَلَا يَبْخَسْ مِنْهُ شَيْئًا ۚ فَإِن كَانَ الَّذِي عَلَيْهِ الْحَقُّ سَفِيهًا أَوْ ضَعِيفًا أَوْ لَا يَسْتَطِيعُ أَن يُمِلَّ هُوَ فَلْيُمْلِلْ وَلِيُّهُ بِالْعَدْلِ ۚ وَاسْتَشْهِدُوا شَهِيدَيْنِ مِن رِّجَالِكُمْ ۖ فَإِن لَّمْ يَكُونَا رَجُلَيْنِ فَرَجُلٌ وَامْرَأَتَانِ مِمَّن تَرْضَوْنَ مِنَ الشُّهَدَاءِ أَن تَضِلَّ إِحْدَاهُمَا فَتُذَكِّرَ إِحْدَاهُمَا الْأُخْرَىٰ ۚ وَلَا يَأْبَ الشُّهَدَاءُ إِذَا مَا دُعُوا ۚ وَلَا تَسْأَمُوا أَن تَكْتُبُوهُ صَغِيرًا أَوْ كَبِيرًا إِلَىٰ أَجَلِهِ ۚ ذَٰلِكُمْ أَقْسَطُ عِندَ اللَّهِ وَأَقْوَمُ لِلشَّهَادَةِ وَأَدْنَىٰ أَلَّا تَرْتَابُوا ۖ إِلَّا أَن تَكُونَ تِجَارَةً حَاضِرَةً تُدِيرُونَهَا بَيْنَكُمْ فَلَيْسَ عَلَيْكُمْ جُنَاحٌ أَلَّا تَكْتُبُوهَا ۗ وَأَشْهِدُوا إِذَا تَبَايَعْتُمْ ۚ وَلَا يُضَارَّ كَاتِبٌ وَلَا شَهِيدٌ ۚ وَإِن تَفْعَلُوا فَإِنَّهُ فُسُوقٌ بِكُمْ ۗ وَاتَّقُوا اللَّهَ ۖ وَيُعَلِّمُكُمُ اللَّهُ ۗ وَاللَّهُ بِكُلِّ شَيْءٍ عَلِيمٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse provides comprehensive guidance for transactions involving future obligations, such as contracting a debt for a specified term. It establishes the principle that such agreements should be put in writing, with a clear timeline for fulfillment. The verse instructs that an impartial scribe should write down the agreement accurately. It emphasizes that anyone with the ability to write—a skill granted by Allah—should not refuse to act as a scribe when asked: “Let no scribe refuse to write as Allah has taught him.” This reflects the context in which the verse was revealed, a time when literacy was rare and skilled scribes were in high demand. The person who owes the debt should dictate the terms, and they are reminded to be mindful of Allah and not to conceal or reduce any part of the obligation. The debtor should dictate the wording of the contract, since it will serve as evidence against them: “And let the one who has the obligation dictate.” This ensures fairness, as the debtor is usually the weaker party. If the creditor dictated, they might attempt to set an unfairly early repayment date, and the debtor—out of hesitation or vulnerability—might not object. If the debtor is unable to dictate due to incapacity or weakness, a guardian or representative should do so fairly. The verse also mandates securing witnesses. The standard is two men; if two men are not available, then one man and two women who are trustworthy may serve. The reasoning is that if one woman forgets, the other can remind her, thus preserving the accuracy of the testimony in legal matters. Witnesses are reminded not to refuse when called, as their testimony helps uphold justice and protect rights. The verse also advises not to consider any debt too small to be documented. Writing it down is fairest in the sight of Allah and helps prevent disputes and misunderstandings. For on-the-spot transactions—where goods and payment are exchanged immediately—documentation is not required, though witnesses are still encouraged. The purpose is to stress the value of recording even routine sales, as is common today through invoices. However, this is not obligatory. Similarly, it is not objectionable if neighbors or shopkeepers do not record every frequent transaction between them. The verse concludes by protecting the roles of scribes and witnesses, warning that they must not be harmed for fulfilling their duties. Doing so is considered a sinful act of disobedience to Allah. The verse ends with the reminder: “And be mindful of Allah, and Allah teaches you.” i.e. to remain conscious of Allah, who has full knowledge of all our actions.

Verse 283

وَإِن كُنتُمْ عَلَىٰ سَفَرٍ وَلَمْ تَجِدُوا كَاتِبًا فَرِهَانٌ مَّقْبُوضَةٌ ۖ فَإِنْ أَمِنَ بَعْضُكُم بَعْضًا فَلْيُؤَدِّ الَّذِي اؤْتُمِنَ أَمَانَتَهُ وَلْيَتَّقِ اللَّهَ رَبَّهُ ۗ وَلَا تَكْتُمُوا الشَّهَادَةَ ۚ وَمَن يَكْتُمْهَا فَإِنَّهُ آثِمٌ قَلْبُهُ ۗ وَاللَّهُ بِمَا تَعْمَلُونَ عَلِيمٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Building upon the detailed instructions in the previous verse—which addressed transactions during periods of settlement—this verse offers guidance for situations that arise while traveling, when a scribe may not be available. In such cases, it permits the use of a pledge (security) delivered by hand as a practical alternative to a written agreement. The verse then discusses cases of mutual trust: if one party trusts the other, the one who is trusted must honor that trust and fulfill it faithfully. Both the person holding the pledge and the borrower are regarded as trustees in their respective roles, and they are urged to act honestly and remain mindful of Allah, their Lord. It continues by emphasizing the seriousness of concealing testimony, warning that this act stains the heart with sin. The verse ends with a clear reminder: Allah has full knowledge of all actions. Nothing is hidden from Him, and all will be accounted for—reinforcing the theme of responsibility before Allah.

Verse 284

لِّلَّهِ مَا فِي السَّمَاوَاتِ وَمَا فِي الْأَرْضِ ۗ وَإِن تُبْدُوا مَا فِي أَنفُسِكُمْ أَوْ تُخْفُوهُ يُحَاسِبْكُم بِهِ اللَّهُ ۖ فَيَغْفِرُ لِمَن يَشَاءُ وَيُعَذِّبُ مَن يَشَاءُ ۗ وَاللَّهُ عَلَىٰ كُلِّ شَيْءٍ قَدِيرٌ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: Just as this surah began with a declaration of the core principles of faith, this verse—and the concluding section of the surah—reaffirms the foundational truths upon which Islam is built. It proclaims the ultimate reality of Allah’s sovereignty and knowledge, declaring that everything in the heavens and the earth belongs entirely to Him. It is thus only fitting that humanity should submit to Him in obedience and devotion. The verse then states that Allah is fully aware not only of our outward actions but also of our inner thoughts and intentions—whether we express them openly or keep them hidden. This serves as a powerful reminder that nothing escapes His knowledge, not even the silent stirrings of the heart. To better grasp this, we can reflect on how the soul processes thoughts and intentions. It moves through several stages: a passing notion, a formed idea, a conscious inclination, deliberation, and finally, firm resolve. These are five stages. While the first four are fleeting or uncertain, it is the fifth—where intention becomes definite—that truly demands our attention. Ultimately, Allah will hold everyone accountable for what lies within, just as He does for outward actions. He forgives whom He wills and punishes whom He wills, according to His perfect wisdom and justice. This verse emphasizes both Allah’s complete authority and His capacity for mercy, reminding us of the balance between accountability and divine forgiveness.

Verse 285

آمَنَ الرَّسُولُ بِمَا أُنزِلَ إِلَيْهِ مِن رَّبِّهِ وَالْمُؤْمِنُونَ ۚ كُلٌّ آمَنَ بِاللَّهِ وَمَلَائِكَتِهِ وَكُتُبِهِ وَرُسُلِهِ لَا نُفَرِّقُ بَيْنَ أَحَدٍ مِّن رُّسُلِهِ ۚ وَقَالُوا سَمِعْنَا وَأَطَعْنَا ۖ غُفْرَانَكَ رَبَّنَا وَإِلَيْكَ الْمَصِيرُ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse outlines the five core beliefs that every Muslim holds: belief in Allah, His angels, His revealed books, all of His messengers, and the certainty of returning to Him. Muslims believe that all prophets—from Adam to Muhammad—conveyed messages from the same Divine source, and they make no distinction between them in terms of faith and respect. It then describes the attitude of true believers: they say, "We hear, and we obey," expressing full submission to Allah’s guidance. Acknowledging their human limitations, they seek Allah’s forgiveness and affirm their ultimate return to Him. This awareness—that life ends in accountability before Allah—shapes the believer’s perspective and behavior. The verse mirrors themes introduced at the beginning of the surah, grounding its conclusion in the same foundational beliefs.

Verse 286

لَا يُكَلِّفُ اللَّهُ نَفْسًا إِلَّا وُسْعَهَا ۚ لَهَا مَا كَسَبَتْ وَعَلَيْهَا مَا اكْتَسَبَتْ ۗ رَبَّنَا لَا تُؤَاخِذْنَا إِن نَّسِينَا أَوْ أَخْطَأْنَا ۚ رَبَّنَا وَلَا تَحْمِلْ عَلَيْنَا إِصْرًا كَمَا حَمَلْتَهُ عَلَى الَّذِينَ مِن قَبْلِنَا ۚ رَبَّنَا وَلَا تُحَمِّلْنَا مَا لَا طَاقَةَ لَنَا بِهِ ۖ وَاعْفُ عَنَّا وَاغْفِرْ لَنَا وَارْحَمْنَا ۚ أَنتَ مَوْلَانَا فَانصُرْنَا عَلَى الْقَوْمِ الْكَافِرِينَ
Translation: not yet
Reflection: This verse offers deep reassurance and emphasizes individual responsibility and Allah’s mercy. It affirms that Allah does not burden any soul with more than it can bear. This is a profound source of comfort for believers, reminding them that Allah fully understands their strengths and limitations. The verse also highlights the principle of personal accountability: each person will be rewarded for the good they do and will bear the consequences of their own misdeeds. Everyone is ultimately responsible for their actions and will stand before Allah individually. Following this framework of justice and divine compassion, the verse transitions into a heartfelt supplication from the believers. They plead with Allah not to hold them accountable for mistakes made out of forgetfulness or error. They also ask not to be tested with heavy burdens like those placed on earlier communities—recognizing the historical consequences faced by those who disobeyed or resisted divine guidance. Furthermore, they beg not to be burdened beyond their capacity, whether physically or spiritually. The supplication continues with a plea for Allah’s pardon, forgiveness, and mercy—acknowledging that ultimate relief and success come only through His grace. It concludes with a declaration of trust in Allah as their Guardian and Protector, and a request for His help against those who oppose the truth. This powerful prayer encapsulates the humility of the believers, their dependence on Allah’s mercy, and their determination to uphold faith with divine support.
